**Summary.** This paper introduces a sophisticated pulse-shaping technique to enhance population transfer fidelity in complex multistate STIRAP systems. By tailoring the driving laser fields based on keeping system eigenenergies parallel to the dark state, the authors achieve error reductions of several orders of magnitude over standard pulses. This method proves highly effective for controlling quantum transitions in atomic ensembles and remains robust even when accounting for environmental decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses high-fidelity quantum control in open quantum systems by optimizing external driving fields. The extension of adiabatic passage techniques to complex, multi-level atomic structures is highly relevant for quantum memory and quantum simulation using trapped atoms or superconducting circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is improving population transfer efficiency in multistate Stimulated Raman Adiabatic Passage (STIRAP) protocols.

**Main result.** Optimized pulse shaping can reduce the population transfer error by several orders of magnitude compared to standard Gaussian pulses, and this improvement remains robust against parameter variations and spontaneous emission.

**Method.** The authors propose a novel pulse-shaping method extending the quasiparallel-eigenenergy approach. This involves analytically designing pump and Stokes field shapes to keep relevant eigenenergies nearly parallel to the dark state during critical interaction periods.

**Model / system.** The system models multistate STIRAP chains, specifically using magnetic sublevels of degenerate angular momenta ($J_g 	o J_e$). The dynamics are governed by time-dependent Hamiltonian evolution under coupling from right- and left-circularly polarized pulses.

**Key observables.** Population transfer error (fidelity), instantaneous eigenenergies ($\eta$), and the dark-bright energy gap separation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pulse shapes, Rabi frequencies ($\Omega_p, \Omega_s$), single-photon detuning ($\Delta$), multiphoton detuning ($\delta$), and the number of states in the chain (odd numbers $N=2n+1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is restricted to odd-numbered state chains because these generically possess a zero-eigenvalue dark state. The approach avoids introducing auxiliary shortcut fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three-state $\Lambda$ configuration, show optimized pulse shapes for various multi-state systems, and present 2D maps comparing population transfer error versus parameter variations (e.g., Gaussian vs. optimized pulses).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the basic three-state STIRAP model to generalize the concept of quasiparallel eigenenergies to arbitrary odd-numbered state chains, deriving analytic pulse prescriptions and validating them through extensive numerical simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a pulse-shaping method for improving population transfer in multistate stimulated Raman adiabatic passage (STIRAP) with nearest-neighbour couplings only. The method extends the quasiparallel-eigenenergy approach, previously used for two- and three-state systems, to multistate chains without introducing additional shortcut fields. The pump and Stokes fields are shaped so that the eigenenergies closest to the dark state remain nearly parallel to it during the central part of the interaction, where nonadiabatic transitions are most likely to occur. We derive analytic prescriptions for the required pulse shapes and apply them to five-, seven-, and nine-state chains formed by magnetic sublevels of degenerate manifolds with angular momenta $J_g\rightarrow J_e=J_g$ and $J_g\rightarrow J_e=J_g-1$ driven by a pair of right- and left-circularly polarized pulses. Numerical simulations show that the optimized pulses can reduce the population transfer error by several orders of magnitude compared to standard Gaussian pulses. The same shapes also improve robustness against variations of the peak Rabi frequency, the single-photon detuning, and the multiphoton detuning. The comparison between the two angular-momentum families shows that the $J_g\rightarrow J_g$ chains are generally more favourable, because their Clebsch-Gordan coefficients keep the relevant bright-state gap larger in the region where the dark state changes most rapidly. Finally, simulations with spontaneous emission show that the optimized resonant pulses remain advantageous in the lossy regime as well.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously re-examines the scaling behavior of the stochastic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation using the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG). By employing a smooth cutoff, the authors successfully identify three distinct universal scaling regimes: KPZ, Edwards-Wilkinson, and an inviscid regime. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding crossover dynamics in nonlinear physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of advanced non-perturbative field theory tools like FRG to nonlinear PDEs provides deep insights into universality classes in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, which is highly relevant for understanding complex dynamics in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to correctly characterize the universal scaling regimes of the one-dimensional stochastic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation by improving upon flawed Renormalization Group (RG) techniques.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes three distinct universal scaling regimes—KPZ ($z=3/2$), Edwards-Wilkinson ($z=2$), and an inviscid regime ($z=1$)—by employing the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG).

**Method.** The core method is the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) framework, which uses a smooth cutoff to derive flow equations for the effective average action, avoiding divergences encountered with sharp cutoffs.

**Model / system.** The model analyzed is the stochastic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation, which describes dynamics in nonlinear field theories relevant to interface growth and pattern formation. It is compared against the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class.

**Key observables.** The full two-point correlation function, characterized by its dynamical exponent $z$ across different momentum/frequency ranges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dynamical exponents ($z=3/2, 2, 1$), the noise strength ($D_{	ext{KS}}$), and the effective viscosity ($
u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the FRG framework with smooth regulators is crucial; previous perturbative Wilsonian RG approaches using sharp cutoffs are deemed invalid.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the fixed points of the 1D KPZ equation, showing stable IR (KPZ) and UV/intermediate regimes (EW, IB). Figure 6 shows the flow of expansion parameters in both standard and specialized p0-DE approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the necessity of FRG over older RG methods. It then derives the flow equations for the KS model within the FRG framework using various approximations (DE, NLO). Finally, it analyzes the resulting flow to identify and characterize the three distinct universal scaling regimes by calculating correlation functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the renormalization group (RG) approach to the one-dimensional stochastic Kuramoto-Sivashinsky (KS) equation and show that previous approaches based on perturbative Wilsonian RG with a sharp cutoff are not valid, even though they yield a qualitatively correct picture. The reason is that taking momentum derivatives while using the sharp cutoff is not well-defined in some cases and leads to intrinsic divergencies. This is a well-known problem of Wilsonian RG, which can be simply cured by using a smooth cutoff, and employing the functional renormalization group (FRG) framework. We establish the flow equations for the KS model within the FRG, and demonstrate that it flows to the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) fixed point at large scales. We then calculate the full two-point correlation function over a wide range of momenta and frequencies. We show that it exhibits three universal scaling regimes that we characterize: the KPZ regime (with dynamical exponent $z=3/2$), the Edwards-Wilkinson regime (with $z=2$) and the recently discovered inviscid regime (with $z=1$). The latter develops over an extended range of large wavenumbers and originates from the vanishing of the effective viscosity. Lastly, we investigate the large-scale behavior of the deterministic KS equation by studying the limit of vanishing noise and we determine the scales where the KPZ regime can emerge in the deterministic case.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a computable operational definition for maximal quantum leakage by proving its equivalence to minimum-error state discrimination. The authors develop an iterative algorithm to calculate this leakage when considering noisy quantum channels. This advances the field by providing concrete mathematical tools to quantify information privacy in quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools (SDP formulation) to quantify information leakage in quantum systems, which is crucial for secure quantum communication protocols and understanding channel capacity limitations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a computable operational interpretation for quantifying 'maximal quantum leakage,' which measures privacy when classical data is encoded into a quantum system.

**Main result.** Maximal quantum leakage has been proven equivalent to minimum-error quantum state discrimination with equal priors, allowing it to be formulated as a solvable Semidefinite Programming (SDP) problem.

**Method.** The authors establish the equivalence via mathematical proofs and develop an iterative algorithm that alternates optimization between input states and measurements for channel analysis.

**Model / system.** The study operates within quantum information theory, analyzing leakage quantified by POVMs, quantum channels ($\mathcal{E}$), and ensembles of quantum states $\{ho_x\}$.

**Key observables.** Maximal Quantum Leakage ($Q(X 	o A)$), minimum-error discrimination probability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of copies ($n$), number of distinct inputs ($N=|X|$), dimension of the Hilbert space ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The equivalence proof relies on constructing optimal measurement sets from arbitrary POVMs; the iterative algorithm is proposed for non-convex optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the monotonic increase of leakage with copies towards a limit. Figure 2 illustrates the convergence of the iterative algorithm for channel leakage over multiple steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines maximal quantum leakage, proves its equivalence to minimum-error state discrimination (Theorem 1), analyzes its behavior for collective measurements (monotonicity and asymptotic limits), and finally extends the framework to develop an iterative algorithm for quantifying leakage through quantum channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximal quantum leakage quantifies privacy against adversaries with arbitrary intentions. In this work, we prove that computing this leakage is equivalent to minimum-error quantum state discrimination with equal priors. This establishes a computable operational interpretation, addressing the previous difficulty in computing maximal quantum leakage. We further analyze the impact of collective measurements on multiple copies of a state, demonstrating that leakage increases monotonically with the number of copies, which leads to explicitly characterizing the maximal leakage in the asymptotic limit. Extending this framework to quantum channels, we develop an iterative algorithm for the jointly designing of input states and measurements. Numerical examples involving collective measurements and the maximal channel leakage demonstrate our theoretical findings.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a comprehensive, fault-tolerant framework to simulate complex quantum many-body dynamics, specifically using the mixed-field Ising model. By co-designing error correction with observable estimation, they establish concrete benchmarks showing that quantum computation can achieve an exponential speedup over classical tensor network methods for simulating non-equilibrium quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the 'quantum advantage' question by providing quantitative engineering targets and a scalable roadmap for fault-tolerant quantum simulation in condensed matter physics, which is highly relevant to AMO and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is establishing a concrete, quantifiable crossover point where fault-tolerant quantum computation surpasses the best available classical simulation algorithms for complex many-body dynamics.

**Main result.** For realistic error rates ($p=10^{-3}$), the authors demonstrate that fault-tolerant quantum simulation of 1D systems requires significantly less time and fewer qubits than classical tensor network methods, projecting minutes or seconds for 2D models under better error control.

**Method.** The work introduces a full-stack framework that co-designs observable estimation protocols with fault-tolerant Quantum Error Correction (QEC) techniques, specifically improving the implementation of non-Clifford rotations to suppress logical errors.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on simulating quantum many-body dynamics using the mixed-field Ising model in both 1D and 2D lattices. The goal is estimating time-evolved expectation values $\langle\psi(t)| O | \psi(t)angle$ under a Hamiltonian $H$.

**Key observables.** Observable expectation value $\langle\psi(t)| O | \psi(t)angle$, physical qubit count, required runtime (hours/minutes), and logical error accumulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rates ($p=10^{-3}, p=10^{-4}$); system sizes (1D up to 100 sites, 2D lattices); target precision $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the feasibility of implementing a hybrid fault-tolerant architecture combining Clifford+T synthesis with optimized rotation state injection.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum-classical runtime crossover for 1D Ising models, schematic diagrams detailing the algorithm-QEC co-design, and error analyses comparing different simulation methods (e.g., PEPS vs. Trotter) on 2D lattices.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the quantum dynamics task; it then details a novel fault-tolerant framework that integrates observable estimation with QEC protocols; finally, it benchmarks this approach against state-of-the-art classical methods (TN/VMC) to quantify the crossover point.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

While quantum computers promise to solve classically intractable problems, identifying the point at which fault-tolerant quantum computation outperforms the best classical algorithms for practical applications remains an outstanding challenge. Here we establish a concrete quantum-classical crossover for quantum many-body dynamics under realistic hardware conditions. We introduce a scalable fault-tolerant framework that combines coherent observable estimation with a space-time-efficient implementation of non-Clifford rotations, suppressing the residual logical errors that limit existing partially fault-tolerant approaches. A benchmark against state-of-the-art tensor-network and variational Monte Carlo algorithms reveals a concrete crossover for mixed-field Ising dynamics at modest system sizes. For a physical error rate of $p=10^{-3}$, fault-tolerant simulation requires approximately 2 hours and $3.7 \times 10^5$ physical qubits for a 100-site 1D system, whereas tensor network approaches would require about 100 years. For 2D models, where rapid entanglement growth limits the classical evolution time, we project quantum runtimes within minutes. A physical error rate of $p=10^{-4}$ leads to at least an order of magnitude reduction in qubit count ($3.1 \times 10^4$ physical qubits) and runtime (minutes for 1D and seconds for 2D). The reduction in quantum runtime arises from our improved rotation-state injection and co-design of quantum error correction and observable-estimation protocols, which jointly suppress logical-error accumulation and reduce sampling overhead. Our results establish a scalable route towards practical quantum advantage and identify quantitative engineering targets for future fault-tolerant architectures.

</details>

**Authors:** Junhyeong An, Soojoon Lee  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15947v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<sub>FIG. 1: Schematic illustration of the EB-composability problem. Let CPEB be the set of ordered pairs (Φ, Ψ) of CP maps such that Φ ◦Ψ is entanglement breaking. (a) An ordered pair of cones (X, Y) is EB- composable precisely when CPEB contains the Cartesian product of the two cones X×Y; in the figure, we identify the ordered pair (X, Y) with this product. (b) In particular, if, in every dimension, an EB- composable pair (X, Y) satisfies PPT ⊆X and PPT ⊆Y, then the PPT squared conjecture holds.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2607.15947_figures/2607.15947_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2: Schematic illustration of our EB-composability results in the qutrit setting. For qutrit CP maps, PPT ⊆UND1 ⊆SP2 [22], and both UND1 × SP2 and SP2 × UND1 are contained in CPEB (see Theorem 4). Consequently, (UND1 × SP2) ∩(SP2 × UND1) = UND1 × UND1 ⊆CPEB (Corollary 7). Since UND1 contains non-PPT maps, PPT × PPT ⊊UND1 × UND1. Moreover, when either component is fixed to be SP2, the largest compatible cone for the other component is exactly UND1 (see Theorem 6).</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2607.15947_figures/2607.15947_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3: Entanglement-swapping obstruction caused by a 1- undistillable link. When a two-qutrit link in UND1 is joined to a link in S2, in either order, every post-selected entanglement swapping outcome is separable. Consequently, the resulting state is separable, and subsequent entanglement swapping cannot generate end-to-end entanglement between A and Z.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the study of entanglement preservation beyond the standard PPT conjecture by analyzing map composition in the qutrit setting. It proves that combining 1-undistillable channels with low-Schmidt number channels results in entanglement breaking. This has direct implications for understanding limitations in quantum repeaters and entanglement swapping protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools for characterizing when quantum operations fail to preserve entanglement, which is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the EB-composability problem, which generalizes the PPT squared conjecture by analyzing whether the composition of two specific cones of quantum channels is always entanglement breaking.

**Main result.** For qutrit systems, the composition of maps from the 1-undistillable cone ($	ext{UND}_1$) and the Schmidt number $\le 2$ cone ($	ext{SP}_2$) is proven to be entanglement breaking in both orders.

**Method.** The authors utilize the Choi–Jamiołkowski correspondence to translate state properties (like separability) into map properties, proving composition theorems for defined cones of completely positive maps.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on quantum channels acting on two-qutrit systems ($d=3$), specifically characterizing the behavior of $	ext{UND}_1$ and $	ext{SP}_2$ cones under map composition.

**Key observables.** Entanglement breaking (EB) property of composed maps, separability ($\in 	ext{SEP}$), and the Schmidt number of Choi matrices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d=3$ (qutrit case); the structure of the two cones $	ext{UND}_1$ and $	ext{SP}_2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are proven specifically for the two-qutrit setting; map composition is compared against general selective measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic for EB-composability, showing $(	ext{PPT}, 	ext{PPT})$ as a special case, and confirm inclusions like $	ext{PPT} \subseteq 	ext{UND}_1 \subseteq 	ext{SP}_2$ in the qutrit setting.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the EB-composability problem, proving composition theorems for $(	ext{UND}_1, 	ext{SP}_2)$ (Theorem 4), establishing the maximality of $	ext{UND}_1$ (Theorem 6), and finally translating these map results to a state-level entanglement swapping condition (Corollary 10).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement swapping is a fundamental operation in quantum repeaters for establishing entanglement between distant parties. The positive partial transpose (PPT) squared conjecture asks whether two PPT entangled links can generate terminal entanglement through entanglement swapping, or equivalently, whether the composition of two PPT maps is always entanglement breaking. Motivated by this conjecture, we investigate the map-composition problem beyond the PPT setting. For qutrit completely positive (CP) maps, we prove that the composition of any CP map whose Choi matrix is $1$-undistillable with any CP map whose Choi matrix has Schmidt number at most two is entanglement breaking in either order. Moreover, we show that the cone of $1$-undistillable CP maps is exactly the largest qutrit cone of CP maps whose composition with every CP map whose Choi matrix has Schmidt number at most two is entanglement breaking in both orders. Finally, although map composition captures only the standard maximally entangled outcome in entanglement swapping, we prove that any $1$-undistillable two-qutrit state and any state of Schmidt number at most two cannot generate terminal entanglement under an arbitrary selective measurement on the intermediate systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reveals that bandwidth mismatch in CVQKD systems creates a security loophole allowing an eavesdropper to extract key information from out-of-band spectral components. By developing a spectral attack model and verifying it with simulations, the authors demonstrate how this leakage necessitates revising security estimates, providing crucial guidelines for designing robust quantum communication hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, practical vulnerability assessment for quantum communication protocols, bridging the gap between idealized theoretical models and real-world hardware limitations like bandwidth mismatch.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses a practical security loophole in Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CVQKD) caused by bandwidth mismatch between the transmitter and receiver.

**Main result.** This loophole allows an eavesdropper to directly obtain raw key information from out-of-band frequency components, significantly lowering the achievable secret key rate compared to conventional estimates.

**Method.** The authors propose a spectral attack scheme based on Fourier analysis of signal spectra and construct a proof-of-principle attack verified through numerical simulations and experimental considerations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Gaussian-modulated coherent states (GMCS) CVQKD, specifically analyzing Local-Oscillator (LLO)-CVQKD systems operating under spectral spreading due to channel imperfections.

**Key observables.** Leaked information ($\chi_{bE}$), secret key rate (estimated vs. modified), and the required signal-to-noise ratio for eavesdropping.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bandwidth mismatch, insertion loss from wavelength splitters, transmission distance (e.g., 25 km), and cutoff frequencies (e.g., 100 MHz to 150 MHz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on asymptotic limits and heterodyne-detection conditions for calculating leaked information; it also models spectral broadening via a spreading kernel $L(f)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Eve's obtained information against security thresholds at various distances/losses, and show the reduction in the secret key rate when accounting for out-of-band leakage.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the bandwidth mismatch loophole using spectral analysis, then develops the mathematical framework for the attack, presents simulations showing quantitative vulnerabilities across varying loss/bandwidths, and concludes by proposing defense strategies involving both system design (bandwidth) and key rate correction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CVQKD) has attracted extensive attention due to its compatibility and low costs. However, bandwidth mismatch exists to varying degrees between the transmitter and receiver. This may prevent frequency components carrying modulation information from being fully perceived by the legitimate party. In this paper, we identify a practical security loophole caused by bandwidth mismatch and propose a corresponding spectral attack scheme. Different from previous approaches that exploit security loopholes to conceal the excess noise introduced by intercept-resend attacks, this scheme can directly obtain raw-key information without introducing additional disturbances. A proof-of-principle attack on a CVQKD system with filtering operation is constructed to verify the feasibility. Experimental results indicate that Eve can obtain enough information to render the system insecure if this practical security loophole is ignored. Based on the identified security loophole, corresponding defense strategies are proposed. This work helps bridge the gap between theoretical models and practical implementations, providing a reference for defense design in practical quantum communication systems.

</details>

### [Absence of Spin-Glass Order on Migdal--Kadanoff Hierarchical Lattices near Three Dimensions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15676v1)

**Authors:** Manaka Okuyama, Masayuki Ohzeki  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15676v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `exotic spin models in light-matter` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that for Ising spin glasses on specific Migdal--Kadanoff hierarchical lattices, spin-glass order is absent by relating the problem to bond percolation. The authors establish a sufficient criterion using exact RG steps, providing strong theoretical evidence contrasting previous numerical estimates regarding critical dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the techniques involving renormalization group flow and critical phenomena are foundational tools used in analyzing quantum phase transitions and many-body systems in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to rigorously determine the existence or absence of spin-glass order in Ising spin glass models on Migdal--Kadanoff (MK) hierarchical lattices, particularly near three dimensions.

**Main result.** The authors provide a sufficient condition based on bond percolation that proves the absence of spin-glass order and stiffness for specific lattice constructions, even when their fractal dimension approaches three from below.

**Method.** The methodology employs an exact Renormalization Group (RG) step combined with a connectivity argument derived from bond percolation theory to analyze the decay of correlations.

**Model / system.** The system is the Ising spin glass model with symmetric binary couplings, studied on MK hierarchical lattices defined by scale factors and branching numbers. The Hamiltonian involves nearest-neighbor interactions across generations of the lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Spin-glass correlation ($\langle \sigma_A \sigma_B angle^2$), stiffness parameter ($	heta$), and vacancy probability ($\Psi(q)$) relative to the percolation threshold.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The branching number ($s$) must be even, and the lattice structure is characterized by its fractal dimension $d = 1 + \log s / \log b$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derived criterion is only sufficient (not necessary) for proving the absence of order; the method relies on discrete couplings and specific RG steps.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the first two generations of the MK hierarchical lattice structure used in the analysis, showing its recursive nature.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a rigorous criterion based on an exact single-step RG transformation that maps the spin glass problem onto bond percolation. The core proof involves comparing induced dilution probabilities against known percolation thresholds to establish the decay of order parameters across generations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive a rigorous sufficient condition for the absence of spin-glass order in the Ising spin glass with symmetric binary couplings on Migdal--Kadanoff (MK) hierarchical lattices with even branching number. The key observation is that a single exact renormalization-group step creates zero effective bonds with positive probability, thereby reducing the problem to bond percolation on the corresponding hierarchical lattice. When the induced dilution exceeds the percolation threshold, both spin-glass order and stiffness are absent at all temperatures, including zero temperature. As a consequence, our criterion gives a rigorous proof that the Ising spin glass on the square lattice does not exhibit a spin-glass phase within the MK approximation. More unexpectedly, by choosing sufficiently large scale factors and branching numbers, we construct MK hierarchical lattices whose fractal dimensions are arbitrarily close to three from below but still exhibit no spin-glass order. This sharply contrasts with numerical estimates obtained for MK hierarchical lattices with relatively small scale factors and branching numbers, which placed the lower critical dimension near \(2.52\).

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, resource-efficient method for performing joint logical measurements in quantum LDPC codes by utilizing only cat states. By introducing a scheduler code, the authors significantly accelerate the process of implementing complex gates like Clifford and Toffoli circuits compared to previous state-of-the-art methods. This work is crucial for advancing the feasibility and speed of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract quantum coding theory, the underlying concepts of resource state management and efficient measurement scheduling are highly relevant to designing practical fault-tolerant quantum computation architectures using physical platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop significantly faster protocols for performing joint logical measurements of multiple commuting Pauli operators within quantum LDPC codes.

**Main result.** The proposed cat-state measurement approach achieves up to a $74	imes$ speed-up over Viterbi measurements for random Clifford circuits, and shows substantial gains for Toffoli gates as well.

**Method.** The authors propose using a scheduler code to manage the joint measurement sequence of $\ell$ commuting operators, relying solely on cat states rather than complex resource states used in prior work.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum LDPC codes, specifically mentioning implementations suitable for platforms like trapped ions or neutral atoms (the 'walking cat architecture'). The operations involve implementing logical Clifford and Toffoli gates via the CliNR partial error correction scheme.

**Key observables.** Speed-up factor compared to Viterbi measurements; Logical Measurement Counts ($	ext{LM}$) and Cat-based Measurement Counts ($	ext{CM}$); Logical Error Rate ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $\ell$ (number of commuting operators), code sizes (Q70, Q102), target error rates ($10^{-10}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that fault-tolerance is not required to achieve the target logical error rate for a specific algorithm, and it utilizes specialized scheduler codes.

**Figures summary.** Table IV compares Logical Measurements (LM) and Cat-based Measurements (CM) across Viterbi gate-based, Viterbi CliNR, and MEDM CliNR protocols for Toffoli circuits and random Cliffords.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of fast logical operations in LDPC codes. It details the proposed cat-state measurement protocol using a scheduler code (MEDM/MECM). The core argument involves comparing this new method against established Viterbi protocols for implementing logical Clifford gates via CliNR, culminating in quantitative speed-up results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum LPDC codes provide a substantial reduction in qubit overhead required for fault-tolerant quantum computation compared to surface code, thanks to their high encoding rate. However, operating simultaneously on multiple logical qubits encoded in the same block is more challenging and may slow down logical operations. Prior work addresses this problem by designing complex resource states to perform logical measurements in LDPC codes. Here, we propose an approach that only consumes cat states. Whereas previous work on cat-based measurements focuses on a single logical measurement, we design a protocol for the joint measurement of $\ell$ commuting logical operators. The key ingredient is the design of a scheduler code determining the measurement sequence and allowing for the decoding of all logical measurement outcomes. Numerical simulations with the LDPC codes Q70 and Q102 of the walking cat architecture show a speed-up of nearly $3\times$ over Viterbi measurements for the measurement of $\ell=20$ commuting logical operators. Combining our fast logical measurements with a new variant of the CliNR partial error correction scheme, we achieve a speed up of up to up to $74\times$ for random Clifford circuits. Our approach also applies to non-Clifford gates, producing a speed up of up to $5\times$ for Toffoli gates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the path integral formalism for quantum mechanics on an arbitrary Lie group G, extending previous work by rigorously handling compact directions via winding number sums. It applies this framework to compute semiclassical approximations up to two-loop order for systems like the Euler-Arnold model. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum dynamics in highly symmetric, non-Euclidean settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques developed for quantizing constrained or geometrically structured systems (like those on Lie groups) are foundational to understanding quantum field theories in curved backgrounds or effective descriptions of many-body dynamics where symmetries play a crucial role.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on developing a rigorous path integral formulation for quantum dynamics occurring on a general Lie group G.

**Main result.** The authors provide explicit, two-loop order semiclassical approximations for propagators and partition functions associated with Euler-Arnold systems on the group manifold.

**Method.** The methodology involves adapting Feynman's path integral approach to the non-commutative setting of a Lie group, utilizing techniques like summing over winding numbers in maximal tori and performing systematic loop expansions.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum mechanics defined on a Lie Group G. The primary physical model analyzed is the Euler-Arnold system, whose dynamics are governed by Hamiltonians derived from quadratic kinetic terms on the group manifold.

**Key observables.** Propagators, partition functions, and semiclassical approximations of transition amplitudes for Euler-Arnold systems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of approximation (up to two-loop), the structure constants of the Lie algebra, and the geometry of the maximal tori within G.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a quadratic kinetic term for simplification in deriving the Lagrangian action, and it addresses complexities arising from compact directions and non-unimodular groups.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes refer to mathematical equations and expansions rather than figure content).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon previous work by first establishing the path integral framework on L^2(G), then deriving the classical dynamics via Hamiltonian/Lagrangian formalisms for Euler-Arnold systems, and finally executing complex two-loop perturbative calculations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We continue our study of quantum dynamics on a Lie group $G$, initiated in arXiv:2512.19840, by building the path integral that governs transition amplitudes in the Hilbert space $L^2(G)$. This relies on the proper handling of both a noncommutative momentum space and the presence of compact directions in $G$. We show that compactness can be handled through a sum over winding numbers in maximal tori of $G$, generalizing the similar sum commonly encountered for path integrals on a circle. As applications, we compute semiclassical approximations of propagators and partition functions of Euler-Arnold systems, up to (and including) two-loop order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and analyzes Combinatorial Holographic Quantum Secret Sharing (CHQSS) within the AdS3/CFT2 correspondence. It introduces key metrics—distance, reconstruction threshold, and secret threshold—to quantify the scheme's robustness against boundary erasures. The research demonstrates how these quantum information properties are dictated by the underlying holographic geometry and its phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects advanced concepts from quantum information theory (secret sharing) directly to gravitational physics via holography, providing a concrete framework for understanding how geometric properties encode quantum security features.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes combinatorial holographic quantum secret sharing (CHQSS) for a bulk subregion in AdS3/CFT2 to understand how logical information is encoded on the boundary while remaining protected from erasures of boundary parts.

**Main result.** They derive relations between the distance, reconstruction threshold, and secret threshold characterizing CHQSS schemes, constructing families including perfect threshold and non-threshold types.

**Method.** The analysis involves defining combinatorial parameters ($d_b, r_b, s_b$) based on holographic entanglement structures (entanglement wedges) in AdS3/CFT2.

**Model / system.** The model is a holographic setup involving $n$ boundary subregions $\{A_1, \dots, A_n\}$ on the boundary of AdS3. The geometry is analyzed using an RT-region graph structure to study entanglement wedges.

**Key observables.** Distance ($d_b$), reconstruction threshold ($r_b$), and secret threshold ($s_b$) are key observables used to quantify the security and encoding quality of the scheme.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of boundary subregions ($n$), the holographic phase, and the specific choice of bulk subregion determine the values of the thresholds and distance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on coarse-graining the holographic geometry using an RT-region graph and assumes that access structures satisfy monotonicity and no-cloning theorems derived from entanglement wedge nesting.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate three distinct holographic phases for $n=3$ boundary subregions, showing reconstructability dependence on phase. Other figures plot parameter values ($d_b, r_b, s_b$) versus distance along a path in the symmetric $n=5$ setup.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces CHQSS, defines characterizing parameters ($d_b, r_b, s_b$), analyzes holographic phase transitions of entanglement wedges, derives relations between these parameters, and finally constructs specific families of CHQSS schemes in the symmetric setting.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce combinatorial holographic quantum secret sharing (CHQSS) for a bulk subregion in AdS$_3$/CFT$_2$ to study how logical information of the bulk subregion is encoded in the boundary and protected from erasures of boundary subregions. We introduce a distance, a reconstruction threshold, and a secret threshold to characterize CHQSS schemes. We present the phase transitions of multipartite entanglement wedges in a symmetric setup and observe multiple distinct phase transition points. The distance and thresholds depend on the holographic phase and the choice of bulk subregion. We derive the maximum distance. Moreover, we derive the relations between the distance and the thresholds. We construct a family of CHQSS schemes in the symmetric setting that includes perfect threshold CHQSS and perfect non-threshold CHQSS.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates that the global fermion parity of an Andreev molecule can be measured locally by observing the nonlocal Josephson effect. By coupling two quantum dots via superconducting junctions, researchers found that changes in the molecular ground-state parity cause a distinct $\pi$-phase shift in the supercurrent response. This establishes the NLJE as a powerful tool for probing fundamental quantum numbers in hybrid superconducting circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental realization of measuring a fundamental quantum number (fermion parity) in superconducting circuits by exploiting non-local transport phenomena, which is highly relevant for understanding topological superconductivity and charge quantization in mesoscopic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to detect the global fermion parity of a delocalized superconducting state using only local measurements.

**Main result.** The nonlocal Josephson effect (NLJE) was shown to manifest characteristic $\pi$-phase shifts in its response, providing an experimentally accessible signature for changes in the system's global fermion parity.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurement of the nonlocal supercurrent modulation with theoretical modeling based on a minimal Andreev-molecule Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an Andreev molecule realized using two coupled quantum-dot Josephson junctions within a carbon nanotube. The model treats superconducting correlations and interdot hybridization effects.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal Josephson response (periodic modulation of critical current $I_{c,L}$ vs. flux bias $\delta R$), and the signature diagnostic is the abrupt $\pi$-phase shift in this nonlocal signal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates at very low temperatures (10 mK) and depends critically on controlling gate voltages to tune between different charge/parity regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors assume that the observed nonlocal transport signatures are directly traceable to the delocalization of Andreev molecular states, which is confirmed by comparing results across different control schemes (Device B vs. Device T).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the device and the parity detection mechanism; key figures show $I_{sw,L}$ modulation as a function of flux bias ($\delta R$) demonstrating NLJE, conductance maps revealing avoided crossings, and theoretical calculations mapping out superconducting parity islands.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical system (Andreev molecule in CNT), then demonstrating the nonlocal Josephson effect via measurements, linking this effect to wavefunction delocalization, and finally using theory/further experiments to confirm that the phase shift directly maps onto global fermion parity changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fermion parity is a fundamental property of superconducting many-body states. Here, we show that the global fermion parity of a delocalized superconducting state can be detected locally by exploiting the nonlocal Josephson effect. Using a carbon nanotube-based Andreev molecule formed by two coupled quantum-dot Josephson junctions, we observe a pronounced nonlocal Josephson response and demonstrate the formation of delocalized Andreev molecular states extending across both junctions. We further show that changes in the molecular ground-state parity manifest as characteristic $π$-phase shifts in the nonlocal response. Supported by a minimal theoretical model, these results identify global fermion parity as an experimentally accessible degree of freedom in hybrid superconducting circuits that can be readily revealed through the nonlocal Josephson effect.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel entropy-ruled theory for charge transport in biomolecules subjected to dynamic disorder. It quantifies the role of degeneracy using differential entropy, showing that this quantum effect can fundamentally alter the expected transport mechanism. The findings suggest that under optimal conditions, charge movement resembles coherent band conduction rather than simple thermally activated hopping.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated quantum mechanical framework to model charge transfer in complex, disordered biological media, bridging concepts from condensed matter physics (band theory) with biophysics (redox chemistry).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses charge transport in biological systems, focusing on how dynamic disorder influences electron transfer rates and mobility across different ordering regimes.

**Main result.** The theory demonstrates that for ultra-fast dynamical and degenerate cases, the electronic mobility transitions from thermally activated hopping to band-like behavior, quantified by differential entropy.

**Method.** It proposes a 'flickering resonance-coupled entropy-ruled charge transport theory' formalism incorporating dynamic disorder effects via differential entropy ($h_S$).

**Model / system.** The system models charge transport in biomolecules with extended hopping sites, relevant for bioenergetics and redox catalysis. The model incorporates coupling between electronic and nuclear dynamics (dynamic disorder).

**Key observables.** Electron transfer rate ($k_{CT}$), diffusion-based mobility ($	ext{D}/\mu$), and the degeneracy-weighted differential entropy ($h_S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dynamic disorder strength, applied electric field ($\vec{E}$), site energy difference ($\Delta E_{ij}$), and the degree of resonance degeneracy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that dynamic disorder can be quantified by differential entropy and models the transition between hopping and band transport regimes using generalized rate equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how electron transfer rates change with applied electric fields, showing charge trapping at high bias in extended systems, and demonstrating mobility enhancement when degeneracy is maximized near zero energy flux.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical formalism by first introducing the concept of entropy-ruled transport, then deriving generalized rate equations that account for dynamic disorder via $h_S$, and finally analyzing the transition between hopping, band, and intermediate regimes under varying external biases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Charge transport (CT) in biological systems is of great interest due to its role in various functional activities like photobiology, bioenergetics, redox catalytic and other metabolic activities, etc. It has been observed by various studies that the presence of a dynamic disorder in biomolecules facilitates charge dynamics in the intermediate transport regime, i.e., far from hopping and towards a bank-like mechanism. Hitherto, the dynamic disorder (in a time scale, coupling between electronic and nuclear dynamics) weightage on CT in molecules is not well-established for the measurement of molecular conductivity from small to long-range ordering. With this motivation, we propose the flickering resonance-coupled entropy-ruled charge transport theory for transfer-rate and diffusion-based mobility (D/μ) calculations. The proposed analytical formalism incorporates the impact of dynamic disorder-correlated degeneracy (in a flickering-resonance manner) on the entropy-ruled electron transfer rate and diffusion-mobility, which are valid for localized hopping, delocalized band transport, and regimes in between. By this approach, it has been observed that the dynamics-driven electronic site matching probability enhances the degeneracy, which is quantified by the differential entropy. The analysis clearly shows that for ultra-fast dynamical and degenerate cases, the entropy-ruled mobility is transformed as band mobility, rather than the thermally activated hopping or diffusion process.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how turbulence statistics, specifically velocity circulation, behave in Rayleigh-Bénard convection compared to idealized homogeneous isotropic turbulence. The authors find that while the statistical signatures of HIT persist within the thermal boundary layer, the local vortex dynamics are strongly influenced by temperature gradients and buoyancy effects. They employ advanced statistical mechanics tools to model the underlying physics governing vortex shape and circulation fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the use of concepts like effective Boltzmann distributions for geometric parameters (aspect ratio) and the analysis of non-equilibrium fluctuations in confined systems share mathematical parallels with approaches used in analyzing quantum many-body systems or complex energy landscapes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate whether statistical properties of velocity circulation observed in Homogeneous Isotropic Turbulence (HIT) persist or are modified in Rayleigh–Bénard convection (RBC).

**Main result.** Circulation statistics within the thermal boundary layer show features akin to HIT, and the Area Rule remains well satisfied; outside this region, behavior resembles HIT governed by energy dissipation.

**Method.** Direct Numerical Simulations (DNS) of Boussinesq equations combined with statistical analysis of circulation probability density functions (PDFs) and vortex geometry.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes turbulent Rayleigh-Bénard Convection (RBC) using the Boussinesq approximation, comparing its statistics to established models for HIT. The analysis focuses on local vortex structures and global circulation fluxes.

**Key observables.** Velocity circulation ($\Gamma[C]$), elementary vortex distribution, vortex aspect ratios ($r$), and standardized Circulation Probability Density Functions (PDFs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rayleigh number ($	ext{Ra} = 10^9$) and Prandtl number ($	ext{Pr} = 0.7$); boundary layer thickness ($\delta T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that circulation fluctuations are dominated by localized vortex spots (Vortex Gas Model hypothesis) and models the aspect ratio distribution using an effective Boltzmann weight.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare exact vs. reconstructed circulation PDFs, show collapse of PDFs for varying contour sizes within the TBL, and illustrate aspect-ratio distributions showing deviations from HIT near walls.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the comparison between RBC and HIT statistics in the TBL; it then analyzes vortex shape correlations with temperature; finally, it develops a theoretical framework using statistical mechanics to model the stationary distribution of vortex aspect ratios.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Important statistical properties of velocity circulation in homogeneous isotropic turbulence (HIT) have been unveiled in recent years, raising the question of whether they persist, or are modified, in other classes of turbulent flows. Motivated by the dominant role of small-scale structures in the circulation fluctuations of HIT, we investigate their relevance in direct numerical simulations of Rayleigh-Bénard convection at a Rayleigh number of $10^9$ and Prandtl number of $O(1)$. Within the thermal boundary layer (TBL), the distribution of elementary vortices is found to be strongly correlated with the temperature field, while the statistics of their core aspect ratios is significantly altered. Additionally, the probability distribution functions of circulation, computed for planar contours which are parallel to the walls, display salient features closely akin to those observed in HIT, with the {\it Area Rule} (a connection between circulation statistics and minimal surfaces) remaining particularly well satisfied -- except for a possible transitional region at a distance of a few TBL thicknesses. Away from the TBL, as expected, the overall statistical behavior of these structures likewise resembles that of HIT, where the intermittent spatial distribution of small vortex tubes is instead determined by the energy dissipation field.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the possibility of measuring relic gravitational wave statistics using photon interference in an optical cavity. By analyzing the coupled action and coherence functions, the authors prove that due to underlying symmetries, the measured Hanbury-Brown Twiss correlations of the photons are completely insensitive to the statistical properties of the gravitons. This places a fundamental limit on what can be inferred about the early universe's gravitational background from current photon measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework demonstrating fundamental limitations on experimental detection methods by proving that certain quantum correlations (graviton statistics) cannot be accessed via simpler measurements (photon intensity correlation).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if Hanbury-Brown Twiss (HBT) correlations measured from photons confined in an optical cavity can reveal the super-Poissonian statistical properties of relic gravitons interacting with them.

**Main result.** The HBT correlations of the cavity photons are insensitive to the second-order coherence of the gravitons, meaning the graviton statistics cannot be inferred from photon intensity measurements.

**Method.** The analysis uses generalized Glauber correlators extended to tensor fields (for gravitons) and electromagnetic fields (for photons), analyzing the total action derived from coupling gravity and electromagnetism.

**Model / system.** The system models the interaction between cosmic relic gravitons (from zero-point fluctuations) and a quantized electromagnetic field confined within a closed optical resonator. The Hamiltonian analysis shows that the cavity photon number operator commutes with the full Hamiltonian, leading to the decoupling of coherence measurements.

**Key observables.** Hanbury-Brown Twiss correlations ($g^{(2)}$), first-order coherence ($g^{(1)}$), and the degree of second-order coherence for both photons and gravitons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cosmic graviton wavenumbers $O(k_p)$ ($\approx 0.002 	ext{ Mpc}^{-1}$); tensor-to-scalar ratio ($r_T$); small coupling limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending Glauber correlators to tensor cases and assumes a conformally flat background metric for the action derivation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in notes, but the structure implies derivations of correlators (Eq. 2.16) and Hamiltonian evolution equations (B.10-B.17).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing generalized Glauber correlators for vector/tensor fields, then applying these to derive the total action coupling photons and gravitons, culminating in a symmetry argument showing that photon coherence is decoupled from graviton statistics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unlike the gravitational waves classically generated by moving macroscopic masses, the diffuse backgrounds of gravitons originate most likely from zero-point fluctuations of the gravitational field amplified by the evolution of the space-time curvature. The resulting entangled states lead to specific second-order correlation effects that could be eventually detected. For a quantitative analysis of this empirical expectation we scrutinize the interactions between the cosmic gravitons and the fundamental mode of a quantized electromagnetic field confined inside a closed optical resonator with perfectly-reflecting walls. We show that the Hanbury-Brown Twiss correlations of the photons are insensitive to the degrees of second-order coherence of the gravitons even barring for the exceedingly small couplings of the problem. Since the degree of second-order coherence of the photons does not reflect the correlation properties of the gravitons, the statistical properties of the gravitons (and their super-Poissonian statistics) cannot be inferred, even in principle, from the intensity correlations of the cavity modes.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors investigate the fundamental limits of estimating multiple parameters characterizing an SU(2) qubit rotation corrupted by noise. They show that the optimized measurement strategy achieves the tight Bayesian Nagaoka-Hayashi bound within numerical precision. This confirms the optimality of the protocol relative to this advanced theoretical limit for the studied channel family.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous test case demonstrating how advanced information-theoretic bounds (like NH) can be numerically verified as achievable in quantum estimation protocols, which is crucial for designing optimal quantum sensors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the problem of multiparameter quantum estimation, aiming to find the minimum achievable error (Bayes risk) when estimating several parameters simultaneously using noisy quantum measurements.

**Main result.** The optimized estimation strategy for a qubit rotation channel under depolarizing noise was shown to attain the Bayesian Nagaoka-Hayashi bound within numerical precision, suggesting this bound is tight for this specific channel family.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as minimizing the weighted Bayesian Mean Square Error (MSE) using Semidefinite Programming (SDP), exploiting rotational symmetry to reduce the complexity of the joint optimization over probe and measurement.

**Model / system.** The system models the single-shot estimation of parameters governing an SU(2) qubit rotation channel that is corrupted by depolarizing noise. The analysis considers $k$ parallel uses of this noisy channel.

**Key observables.** Bayes risk ($ar{\epsilon}^*$), Bayesian Nagaoka-Hayashi bound ($ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{NH}}$), and the SLD Cramér-Rao bound ($ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{SLD}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Depolarizing noise strength ($\lambda$) and the number of parallel uses ($k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the class of parallel ancilla-assisted quantum strategies, and the attainment of the NH bound remains an open theoretical problem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the achieved risk ($ar{\epsilon}^*$) against the two theoretical bounds ($ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{SLD}}$ and $ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{NH}}$) as a function of noise strength ($\lambda$) for $k=1$ to $4$, showing the convergence of curves.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the hierarchy of quantum estimation bounds, formulates the minimization problem via SDPs exploiting rotational symmetry, computes integrals exactly using quadrature methods, and compares the achieved risk against these theoretical limits across varying noise levels and uses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Bayesian Nagaoka--Hayashi (NH) bound is a semidefinite lower bound on the Bayes risk of multiparameter estimation, tighter than the Bayesian symmertic logarithmic derivative (SLD) Cramér--Rao bounds, and whether it can be attained is an open problem. We study the single-shot estimation of a qubit rotation, all three parameters at once, under depolarizing noise and a uniform prior, with $k$ parallel uses of the channel. Rotational covariance reduces the joint optimization of probe, measurement, and estimator, and the NH bound itself, to small semidefinite programs, solvable through $k=4$. At every number of uses and noise strength the optimized strategy reaches the NH bound within the numerical precision of the calculation, numerical evidence that the bound is attained for this channel family, while the SLD bound lies strictly below and is never attained. As the noise grows the optimal probe collapses to the tensored Bell state.

</details>

### [Agnostic learning of qudit stabilizer states](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15559v1)

**Authors:** Wentao Qi, Boyan Xu, Shiguang Feng, Lvzhou Li

**Summary.** This paper introduces an efficient quantum algorithm to estimate the best approximation of an unknown qudit state within the set of stabilizer states. It achieves this by generalizing a known qubit technique, overcoming structural hurdles specific to higher dimensions using advanced sampling methods. This advancement is vital for developing robust quantum error correction and characterizing complex quantum states in qudit systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends fundamental quantum information protocols from qubits to higher-dimensional qudits, which is crucial for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation using physical systems that naturally support qudit encoding (e.g., superconducting circuits or photon polarization).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is agnostic learning of stabilizer states, which involves finding the best approximation of an unknown quantum state $|\psiangle$ within the restricted set of stabilizer states $S$, especially when noise corrupts the state.

**Main result.** The authors present the first efficient quantum algorithm for this task in qudit systems ($d>2$), successfully generalizing a known qubit framework. The algorithm achieves high fidelity estimation with complexity scaling polynomially in system parameters and inversely with the target fidelity $	au$.

**Method.** The technique generalizes the stabilizer bootstrapping framework using skewed Bell difference sampling, which is necessary because standard methods fail for qudits. This involves iterative refinement based on measuring correlations over Weyl operators.

**Model / system.** The system under study consists of $n$-qudit pure states $|\psiangle$ in a dimension $d$, where $d$ is assumed to be an odd prime. The target set of states are the stabilizer states, which are central to quantum error correction theory.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer Fidelity ($	ext{FS}(|\psiangle)$), $|\langle\phi|\psiangle|^2$, and correlation measurements $\left|\langle\psi| W_x |\psiangleight|^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $d$ (odd prime dimension), $n$ (number of qudits), $	au$ (target fidelity threshold), and $\epsilon$ (approximation error).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the dimension $d$ is an odd prime, which allows for the use of skewed Bell difference sampling. The analysis also relies on establishing anti-concentration properties for certain marginal distributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the quantum circuit required for the SWAP test between two $n$-qudit states; other figures detail the transformation process using Clifford gates in a related context (Chunk 3 notes).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first addressing the generalization of the learning framework to qudits, detailing the necessary sampling techniques (skewed Bell difference sampling), and then presenting the complexity bounds for state estimation and magic number calculation. A separate section details circuit construction rules for subspace transformations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning a classical description of a quantum state is a fundamental task in quantum computation. Among the most important classes of quantum states are stabilizer states, which play a central role in quantum error correction and fault-tolerant computation. To mitigate the effects of realistic noise, agnostic learning of stabilizer states has emerged as a natural and well-motivated problem. Recently, Chen \textit{et al.} [STOC'25, p. 429-438] resolved this problem for qubit systems by using a stabilizer bootstrapping framework. However, the agnostic learning of qudit stabilizer states remains largely unexplored, since the qudit setting introduces fundamental structural differences that prevent a direct generalization of existing qubit techniques. In this paper, we successfully generalize the stabilizer bootstrapping framework to qudit systems and present the first efficient quantum algorithm for agnostic learning of qudit stabilizer states. Specifically, given copies of an unknown $n$-qudit pure state $|ψ\rangle$ that has fidelity $τ$ with some stabilizer state, our algorithm outputs a stabilizer state $|φ\rangle$ such that $\left| \braket{φ|ψ} \right|^2 \geq τ- \varepsilon$ with high probability. The algorithm uses only single-copy and four-copy measurements, and its sample and time complexity scale as $(d/τ)^{O(d^2 \log(1/τ))} \cdot \mathrm{poly}(n, 1/\varepsilon)$, where the dimension $d$ is an odd prime. As a direct corollary, our algorithm enables efficient estimation of the magic of a quantum state, as quantified by its stabilizer fidelity. Completing the picture, we also present a streamlined algorithm for the high-fidelity regime $τ> \cos^2(π/8)$, establishing a qudit analogue of the threshold-based approach in prior qubit work.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores altermagnetism—a magnetic state characterized by specific symmetries—in models lacking conventional long-range order. Using advanced Green's function techniques, the authors demonstrate that a unique 'altermagnetic liquid' phase can exist at finite temperatures. This finding expands the understanding of correlated electron physics beyond traditional magnetically ordered states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of emergent order parameters (like spin-pseudospin correlation) existing without breaking translational symmetry is highly relevant to understanding quantum criticality in complex correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how altermagnetic behavior can emerge in quantum materials without requiring conventional long-range magnetic or orbital order.

**Main result.** The authors establish the concept of an 'altermagnetic paramagnet' by showing that a composite state with nonzero spin-pseudospin correlations exists above a critical coupling, even when average spins are zero.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis employing the rotation-invariant Green's function method (RGM) to calculate correlation functions and excitation spectra.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using the Kugel–Khomskii spin–pseudospin model, an SU(2) x SU(2) symmetric Hamiltonian applied to lattices (square lattice and linear chain).

**Key observables.** Spin-spin and spin-pseudospin correlation functions, excitation spectra (acoustic and optical branches), heat capacity, and susceptibilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical intersubsystem exchange coupling Kc(T); temperature T; and the ratio of various exchange couplings J, I, and K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the RGM approach to handle finite-temperature effects while maintaining zero single-site average magnetization ($\langle \mathbf{S}_i angle = 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate correlation functions showing non-zero values at critical points, heat capacity exhibiting step changes, and excitation spectra splitting into distinct acoustic and optical branches.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model on 2D and 1D lattices, deriving Green's function expressions for various correlations and excitations, culminating in a phase diagram mapping out the transition to the altermagnetic liquid state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kugel-Khomskii spin-pseudospin model, originally developed for transition-metal compounds with orbital degrees of freedom, has recently been reinterpreted in the context of altermagnetism. In this work, we theoretically investigate the emergence of altermagnetic behavior in the absence of long-range magnetic or orbital order. Using the rotation-invariant Green's function method for the SU(2) x SU(2) symmetric model on a square lattice and on a linear chain, we analyze spin-spin and spin-pseudospin correlation functions, excitation spectra, heat capacity, and susceptibilities. We show that beyond a critical intersubsystem exchange Kc(T), a composite state arises with nonzero spin-pseudospin correlations, even though the average spin and pseudospin at each site are zero. The excitation spectrum splits into acoustic and optical branches, with nodal lines along qx = qy - a direct signature of altermagnetic symmetry. A peak in heat capacity and a jump in susceptibility are observed at the phase boundary. In 1D, the phase boundary is nonmonotonic and demonstrates reentrant transition. These results establish the concept of an "altermagnetic paramagnet" or "altermagnetic liquid" without long-range order, relevant for low-dimensional and strongly fluctuating systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC), a novel sampler that learns stochastic paths and corrects their statistical weights using non-equilibrium work derived from path ratios. It demonstrates that NHMC significantly improves sampling quality for complex systems like lattice $\phi^4$ models by providing robust estimators for free energies and observables even when direct endpoint densities are hard to calculate.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of non-equilibrium fluctuation theorems to machine learning proposals provides a powerful, theoretically grounded way to improve sampling efficiency in many-body physics simulations beyond standard HMC or simple flow matching.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is accurately sampling from unnormalized Boltzmann densities, which is difficult for classical methods when modes are separated or proposals fail to preserve the target distribution.

**Main result.** The proposed Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC) method successfully generates corrected estimates of free energies and expectation values by utilizing recorded non-equilibrium work derived from path ratios.

**Method.** NHMC is a 'train-then-correct' learned Hamiltonian sampler that learns stochastic paths toward the target distribution. Corrections are achieved using identities related to non-equilibrium work (like Jarzynski's equality) applied via Path-SNIS or path-IMH sampling.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on various physical targets, including double-well potentials ($\phi^4$ models), finite-volume lattice $\phi^4$ systems, and molecular internal coordinates (e.g., Ala4, Ala6).

**Key observables.** Action overlap, log-weight spread, integrated autocorrelation time ($	au_{	ext{int}}$), free-energy differences, magnetization ($|M|$), and susceptibility ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Path overlap sufficiency is critical; the method performs well when sufficient path overlap exists between proposal and reference paths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the mutual absolute continuity of the forward and reverse path laws, and performance degrades significantly if path overlap is poor.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate NHMC path construction and recorded-work corrections; specific tables compare $\phi^4$ observables (like $|M|$ and $\chi$) across different sampling chains (HMC vs. round-trip NHMC-MH) and show action overlap improvements.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the theoretical framework of NHMC using non-equilibrium work, derives estimators like Path-SNIS, validates it on simple potentials (DW), and then applies and compares its performance against established methods (HMC, MD) across complex lattice ($\phi^4$) and molecular systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sampling from an unnormalized Boltzmann density requires proposals that move probability mass globally while retaining enough path-probability information for statistical correction. We introduce Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC), a train-then-correct learned Hamiltonian sampler. Starting from a tractable base distribution, NHMC learns stochastic Hamiltonian-style paths toward the target. Once training is complete, the learned proposal parameters are fixed; the proposal then generates complete paths and endpoint configurations, which are statistically corrected using the recorded non-equilibrium work. This dimensionless generalized work is determined by the probability ratio between the forward proposal path and a reverse reference path. During training, minimizing its mean reduces a path-space KL divergence and controls an upper bound on endpoint mismatch. During evaluation, the same quantity defines weights for self-normalized importance sampling on paths (path-SNIS), estimates normalizing constants or free-energy differences, and gives the acceptance ratio for path-space independent Metropolis-Hastings (path-IMH). The same forward-reverse laws also define a shared-bridge round-trip Metropolis kernel that acts directly on configurations and preserves the Boltzmann target. On double-well and finite-volume lattice $φ^4$ targets, the NHMC construction gives corrected estimates when path overlap is sufficient; when overlap is poor, weight degeneracy, low acceptance, and long autocorrelation expose proposal failure. We additionally report a molecular internal-coordinate feasibility study using an MD prior and learned-force path proposal.

</details>
