**Summary.** This paper proposes using Grover's quantum search algorithm to accelerate the edge detection process in digital images. By restricting the problem to small $2 	imes 2$ blocks, they demonstrate a method robust enough for current noisy quantum hardware simulations. The work shows that this quantum approach offers high success probability compared to existing methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies a fundamental quantum algorithm (Grover's) to a practical signal processing problem. For AMO physicists interested in quantum computation, it serves as an example of mapping physical measurement tasks onto quantum circuits suitable for near-term devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is performing edge detection on a digital image, which requires locating gradients across pixel intensities.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using Grover's algorithm provides a robust method for quantum edge detection with high success probability, outperforming other quantum approaches tested on current NISQ devices.

**Method.** They adapt Grover's search algorithm to find the marked 'edge pixels' within an image block, simulating this process using Qiskit circuits.

**Model / system.** The model is a digital image ($L_1 	imes L_2$ lattice) whose edge detection problem is mapped onto a quantum search problem. The implementation is restricted to small $2 	imes 2$ blocks for compatibility with NISQ hardware.

**Key observables.** The measurement result $ho_i = |\langle t_i|\psi_fangle|^2$, which indicates the presence of an edge pixel with high probability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The search space size $N$ (total pixels in a block) and the number of marked elements $M$ (edge pixels). The simulation uses qubits for small blocks ($N=4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that Grover's algorithm can be effectively applied to image processing tasks. A major limitation noted is the necessity of dividing the large image into smaller, manageable blocks.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the division of a $4 	imes 4$ lattice into four $2 	imes 2$ blocks. Figure 2 shows the Qiskit circuit for Grover's search on $N=4$. Figure 3 compares sample edges from numerical analysis, simulation on $2 	imes 2$ blocks, and noisy device simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces edge detection as a problem solvable by quantum search. It details the adaptation of Grover's algorithm for this task, presents the implementation using small image blocks ($2	imes 2$), and validates the results through comparison with classical numerical analysis and noisy hardware simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Grover's algorithm is one of the most significant quantum algorithms, enabling the search for a marked element in an unsorted database quadratically faster than classical search algorithms. We utilize Groves's algorithm to detect the edges of a digital image faster than any classical edge detection methods. The high success probability of our method in detecting image edges, compared to most existing quantum edge detection methods, makes it a more suitable candidate for use in the presence of noise on current quantum devices. We implemented our model for the $2\times 2$ blocks of the image using a two-qubit Qiskit circuit with the QASM simulator and the noisy IBM Sydney(fake) device, making it suitable for most of the current NISQ devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the relationship between non-Gaussianity and the magic resource in cat codes, comparing their respective geometric structures. While finding global incompatibility with the established GKP equivalence, the authors prove a constructive local alignment exists asymptotically within the large-separation limit. This suggests a geometry-based approach for understanding quantum resource correspondences.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a geometric framework for understanding resource relations (like NG and magic resources) beyond specific encoding schemes, suggesting general principles applicable to quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the correspondence between non-Gaussianity (NG) and the magic resource, established for GKP encoding, holds true in cat codes.

**Main result.** Cat codes globally fail to inherit the GKP equivalence, but their asymptotic Wigner logarithmic negativity geometry allows for a constructive local alignment with the magic measure geometry under an SU(d) code.

**Method.** The authors compare the Wigner logarithmic negativity (WLN) and a phase-operator based magic measure by analyzing $d$-peaked cat states in the large-separation asymptotic limit.

**Model / system.** The system involves continuous-variable quantum states, specifically non-degenerate $d$-peaked cat states $|\psi_dangle$, analyzed within the context of GKP and SU(d) encoding frameworks.

**Key observables.** Wigner logarithmic negativity (WLN), Magic measure ($M$), Geometric error ($E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed in the large-separation regime, $|\alpha_i - \alpha_j| \gg 1$, and involves parameters related to the number of peaks $d$ and amplitudes $|c_i|$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results rely heavily on the non-degenerate large-separation limit for cat states, which simplifies the Wigner function analysis significantly.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the asymptotic upper bound of WLN for $d$-component cat states and show how finite $|\alpha|$ landscapes converge to this ideal asymptotic structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the GKP equivalence, then analyzes the cat code setting using WLN vs. magic measure comparison. It proves global incompatibility but demonstrates local alignment based on shared phase-rotation invariance in the large-separation limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Gaussianity is an essential resource for genuine quantum advantages in continuous-variable quantum systems and is regarded as a counterpart of the magic resource in discrete-variable systems. Recently, an exact correspondence between non-Gaussianity (NG) and the magic resource was identified within the Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill (GKP) encoding framework. Whether such a relation persists beyond GKP encoding, however, remains unclear. Here, we address this question in the cat-code setting. By comparing the Wigner logarithmic negativity (WLN) and a magic measure defined from a phase-operator basis, we analyze the resource geometry of non-degenerate $d$-peaked cat states. We find that cat codes do not inherit the global value-preserving GKP magic--NG equivalence, but their asymptotic WLN geometry allows a constructive local alignment with the magic-measure geometry. Specifically, under a distinguished SU($d$) asymptotic cat code, we establish a sector-dependent alignment between WLN level sets and magic-measure level sets based on their intrinsic local geometries. These results identify both the global incompatibility and the locally constructive relation between magic and non-Gaussian resources in cat codes, suggesting a geometry-based approach to resource correspondences beyond the GKP framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous inverse engineering technique to determine the exact time-dependent electric field required to realize any desired single-qubit quantum gate. By solving the system's equations of motion and minimizing an associated cost functional, the authors provide optimal pulse shapes that ensure high fidelity while remaining analytically tractable.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, analytically derived recipes for quantum control, which is crucial for experimental realization of quantum gates in platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits where precise microwave/laser pulses are used.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is designing the precise time-dependent control pulse required to implement an arbitrary single-qubit quantum gate using only a linearly-polarized external field.

**Main result.** The authors provide general closed analytical formulas for the control field, demonstrating that optimal pulses can be found by minimizing a cost functional (fluency) over free parameters derived from solving the system's equations of motion.

**Method.** They employ an inverse engineering approach: deriving the required time-dependent Hamiltonian evolution operator and then inverting the resulting differential equations to determine the necessary field amplitude and phase functions.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-level quantum system (qubit) governed by a Hamiltonian $H(t) = -\omega_0/2 \sigma_z - \mu E(t)\sigma_x$. The evolution is analyzed using the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA).

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), control field amplitude $F(t)$, detuning $\Delta(t)$, and pulse fluency ($J$, a cost functional).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial/final state parameters of the target gate, such as $\phi_{ga}$ or $\delta^2$, which act as free parameters to optimize performance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis fundamentally relies on the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA). Furthermore, achieving analytical tractability often requires assuming specific functional forms for intermediate dynamical functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare numerical simulations against derived analytical functions for implementing gates like $i\sigma_x$ and $i\sigma_z$, while Figure 5 maps the pulse fluency cost as a function of various free parameters to identify optimal control settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving general formulas for the control field using dynamical functions, then applies this framework to specific gates ($i\sigma_x$, $i\sigma_z$), and finally optimizes the resulting pulse parameters by minimizing a cost functional (fluency) via numerical comparison with full Hamiltonian simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we address the problem of designing single-qubit quantum gates by means of a linearly-polarized field. We show that any desired one-qubit gate corresponding to a special unitary matrix can be generated by a modulated sinusoidal field. The only approximation involved is the rotating-wave-approximation. The formula for the control field is obtained by inverting the equation of motion for the evolution operator and imposing the conditions for the desired gate. We give a simple procedure to obtain closed analytical formulas for the fields in terms of a priori chosen dynamical functions. Additionally, these dynamical functions can depend on tunable free parameters intentionally introduced to meet a desired performance criteria.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the electronic structure of twisted bilayer WSe2 to understand its superconducting properties by incorporating band topology. Using DFT and partial Wannierization, the authors confirm the bands are topologically non-trivial and construct an effective Hamiltonian based on two derived molecular orbitals. This provides a crucial theoretical benchmark for studying correlated electron physics in moiré TMDs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous *ab initio* foundation for modeling strongly correlated systems using topological band theory, which is highly relevant for understanding unconventional superconductivity in 2D materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether band topology plays an essential role in understanding the correlation physics, particularly superconductivity, observed in twisted bilayer WSe2.

**Main result.** The top moiré valence bands are found to be topologically non-trivial, carrying Chern numbers C=(+1,+1) for the K valley. The authors successfully derived an effective low-energy Hamiltonian using compact molecular orbitals.

**Method.** The study combines first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations with a partial Wannierization procedure guided by symmetry representations to construct localized molecular orbitals.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted bilayer WSe2, modeled via its moiré bands. The effective Hamiltonian is constructed in a $2 	imes 2$ basis spanned by two derived orbitals: a compact 'f' orbital and a topological 'c' orbital.

**Key observables.** Chern numbers (C), hopping amplitudes ($t_{	ext{f}}$), hybridization strengths ($V_{	ext{hyb}}$), and on-site Coulomb interaction ($U$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angles ($	heta$), moiré lattice constant ($a_M$), and the ratio $t_f/U$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing compact molecular orbitals via partial Wannierization, which separates the physics into a localized component ('f') and a topological obstruction ('c').

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures showing Chern numbers; spatial distributions show orbital localization patterns at high-symmetry points ($\gamma, \kappa$); and plots track hopping parameters and hybridization strength as functions of twist angle.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from DFT calculation of moiré bands to identifying their topological nature (Chern numbers). It then employs a partial Wannierization technique guided by symmetry to construct an effective Hamiltonian in terms of two molecular orbitals, allowing for the extraction of key hopping and interaction parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent observations of superconductivity in twisted bilayer WSe$_2$ (tWSe$_2$) have motivated theoretical proposals for unconventional pairing mechanisms. A central question is whether band topology plays an essential role in the system's correlation physics. In this letter, we develop a first-principles-based description of the top moiré valence bands in tWSe$_2$. Using density functional theory (DFT) calculations, we identify the bands in the relevant range of twist angles to be topologically non-trivial, with the top valence bands carrying Chern numbers $C=(+1,+1)$ for the $K$ valley. In order to treat the strong correlation physics, we construct compact molecular orbitals directly from the DFT wave functions through a partial Wannierization procedure and with the guidance of spinful $C_{3z}$ symmetry representations. This yields a localized $f$ orbital together with a complementary topological $c$ orbital, allowing us to extract hopping and hybridization amplitudes from first principles. The resulting parameters provide an ab initio benchmark for the effective Hamiltonian. Our work establishes a foundation for understanding superconductivity in moiré TMDs and highlights tWSe$_2$ as a promising platform for exploring topological superconductivity.

</details>

Algorithms 1 and 3 exit the space of the valid process matri- ces during the optimization, but stay in the space of density matrices because they conserve the spectrum. Algorithm 2 does not conserve the spectrum during the procedure, and therefore it can exit the space of density matrices.</sub>

**Summary.** The paper investigates whether definite causal order can emerge solely from the spectrum of a process matrix in higher-order quantum theory. By analyzing how unitaries transform these matrices, the authors demonstrate that generic processes approach a causally ordered state in the thermodynamic limit. This suggests a fundamental mechanism for classical causality arising purely from algebraic constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles fundamental questions about the emergence of physical principles (like causality) from purely mathematical or algebraic structures, which resonates with foundational studies in quantum information and open systems theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining if definite causal order (the 'when') can emerge purely from algebraic ingredients, specifically from the spectrum of a process matrix, mirroring how locality has been shown to emerge in quantum mereology.

**Main result.** In the thermodynamic limit, generic quantum processes are shown to admit a preferred decomposition into agents that brings the process close to a causally separable state, suggesting an emergence mechanism for classical causality from algebraic structure alone.

**Method.** The authors use spectral analysis and unitary transformations on the process matrix $W$ within the framework of higher-order quantum theory. They derive necessary conditions (like divisibility constraints) that must be met if a process is to possess a definite causal order $\mathcal{O}$.

**Model / system.** The model uses the process-matrix framework for higher-order quantum theory, describing interactions between multiple agents (A, B, etc.). The system's structure is encoded in the process matrix $W$, which acts on a total Hilbert space formed by tensor products of input and output spaces for each agent.

**Key observables.** The spectrum ($	ext{spec}(W)$) of the process matrix $W$ is the primary diagnostic tool, as it is basis-independent. Other observables include spectral distance metrics ($C(\mathbf{w})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the thermodynamic limit and the structure imposed by sequential agent interactions (e.g., $	ext{A} \prec 	ext{B}$). Dimension constraints, such as $d_{k,O} | d_{k+1,I}$, are crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes that defining an agent minimally involves grouping input and output Hilbert spaces, and it relies on dimension divisibility assumptions for the sufficiency of spectral conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how redefining agents via a unitary $U$ can make non-causal processes appear causal. Figure 2 details the numerical approach to finding such unitaries by minimizing a spectral distance cost function.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the problem (causality vs. spectrum) to deriving necessary spectral constraints for fixed order, then showing that these constraints can be satisfied constructively via specific unitary transformations in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In Hamiltonian systems, the only basis-independent quantity is the spectrum. Given a spectrum, quantum mereology seeks preferred tensor-product decompositions into local subsystems, leading to the emergence of locality. Causal order, however, remains fixed by the Hamiltonian time evolution. In contrast, higher-order quantum theory permits more general processes that need not possess a definite global causal order. In the process-matrix framework, specifying a process requires a choice of subsystems corresponding to the input and output Hilbert spaces of each agent. A change of basis redefines both the agents and the corresponding decompositions into subsystems, while leaving the spectrum of the process matrix invariant. Here, we study how causality arises from this spectrum. First, we derive spectral constraints on processes compatible with definite causal order. Second, we show that, in the thermodynamic limit, generic quantum processes admit a preferred decomposition with a definite causal order. This suggests a mechanism for the emergence of classical causality only from algebraic ingredients.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the ambiguity in defining quantum control flow by distinguishing between controlled operations and the quantum SWITCH. It introduces a novel quantum programming language enforced by a linear type system to correctly model the quantum SWITCH over arbitrary noisy channels. This provides a mathematically sound framework for writing complex quantum algorithms involving conditional logic.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous foundational tools for programming with general open quantum systems, which is crucial for simulating realistic physical processes involving dissipation or measurement.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper distinguishes between 'controlled operations' and the 'quantum SWITCH,' arguing they are fundamentally different control mechanisms in quantum computation.

**Main result.** It develops a novel, well-behaved quantum programming language with a linear type system capable of expressing the quantum SWITCH over general quantum channels.

**Method.** The authors use semantic analysis based on program transformations and develop both denotational and operational semantics to formalize the control flow structures.

**Model / system.** The model is abstract quantum computation using qubits, quantum channels (represented by CPTP maps/Kraus operators), and programming language constructs like quantum conditional branching ($	ext{qif}$).

**Key observables.** Well-definedness of control mechanisms; preservation of semantics under translation between formalisms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Linearity constraints, the structure of Kraus decompositions, and the distinction between unitary vs. general channel operations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that controlled operations and the quantum SWITCH must be treated as distinct control primitives to maintain mathematical rigor for arbitrary channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the definition of controlled operations, the syntactic dilation rules mapping constructs from $	ext{QUGCL}'$ to $	ext{QifUnitary}$, and the semantics of various control flow structures using Kraus operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical distinction between quantum control types, develops a formal language framework incorporating linearity constraints, and proves semantic preservation theorems by translating constructs between different formalisms (e.g., $	ext{Vac}$ to $	ext{QifUnitary}$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In contrast to a classical bit, which can only take the value $0$ or $1$, its quantum counterpart -- a qubit -- can exist in a superposition of $0$ and $1$. This is a superposition of data values, naturally raising the question of whether one can superpose not only data but also programs. For example, a particular superposition of programs, known as the quantum SWITCH, has attracted much attention, and its implementations and computational advantages have been studied extensively within the physics community.   A naive way to control a program by a qubit is by means of a controlled operation. Given an operation $F$, this amounts to considering an operation that behaves as $F$ when the control qubit is $|1\rangle$, and as the identity operation when the control qubit is $|0\rangle$. This idea works well when $F$ is a unitary operation, but it is not well-defined for a general quantum channel. By contrast, the quantum SWITCH is free from the well-definedness issue. This contrast leads to the key insight of this paper: controlled operations and the quantum SWITCH should be regarded as different kinds of quantum control mechanisms.   Building on this insight, we develop a novel quantum programming language with quantum control and measurement that can express the quantum SWITCH over quantum channels. Using a semantic analysis based on program transformations, we identify the source of the ill-behavedness of controlled operations as the \emph{correspondence problem}: a lack of coordination between the measurements performed in the then- and else-branches of quantum conditional branching. We address this problem with a linear type system that enforces alignment of the quantum operations used in the two branches, yielding a well-behaved language capable of expressing the quantum SWITCH.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work examines how thermal fluctuations can induce quasi-long-range order in RVB states on the checkerboard lattice. By showing that decoherence suppresses destructive quantum interference, the system's correlations transition from an expected exponential decay to a power-law decay characteristic of dimer models. The paper rigorously proves the mathematical basis for this state using advanced techniques involving local projector operators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of thermal order by disorder, where temperature drives a system into an ordered phase by suppressing quantum interference effects, is highly relevant for understanding exotic phases in strongly correlated electron systems like high-Tc superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates a novel mechanism for 'thermal Order by Disorder' within Resonating Valence Bond (RVB) states on the checkerboard lattice.

**Main result.** Thermal decoherence suppresses destructive quantum interference, causing singlet-singlet correlations to decay with a power law ($1/r^2$) rather than exponentially, indicating quasi-long-range order.

**Method.** The authors derive an effective local spin Hamiltonian and analyze correlation functions by comparing the pure RVB state (exponential decay) against the mixed state resulting from thermal decoherence (power-law decay).

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 model on the checkerboard lattice, modeled using Resonating Valence Bond (RVB) states and related to Quantum Dimer models. The Hamiltonian involves complex local projectors and twelve-spin interactions.

**Key observables.** Singlet–singlet correlation function $C(r)$ and dimer–dimer correlations; the diagnostic is the transition from exponential decay to power-law decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the temperature regime $K \gg T \gg J$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conclusion regarding power-law decay hinges on the linear independence of Valence Bond (VB) states, which is established through a detailed patch expansion procedure assuming periodic boundary conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate mappings between singlets and six-vertex configurations, show the action of plaquette operators, and detail the iterative process of proving linear independence by expanding patches on the lattice.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the Hamiltonian and RVB state to analyzing correlation functions in different regimes. It then dedicates significant effort to mathematically proving the linear independence of singlet coverings via a patch expansion procedure using local projector operators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive a local spin-1/2 Hamiltonian with a resonating valence bond ground state on the checkerboard lattice. The state is characterized by the exponential decay of singlet-singlet correlations, whereas dimer-dimer correlations decay with a power law in the corresponding Quantum Dimer model. This observation leads to a novel mechanism for thermal Order by Disorder whereby thermal decoherence suppresses destructive quantum interference between different contributions to the correlations in the ground state and results in a qualitatively different, quasi-long-range ordered mixed state.

</details>

**Summary.** This study experimentally investigates field-induced transitions in the Kitaev candidate BaCo2(AsO4)2. Using neutron scattering and spin transport measurements, the authors find evidence for first-order phase coexistence rather than a continuous quantum liquid evolution. They conclude that observed excitations are better explained by bound magnon pairs, challenging previous interpretations of anomalous thermal transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it uses advanced spectroscopic and transport techniques to test the boundaries between exotic quantum states (QSLs) and conventional magnetically ordered phases, providing crucial insights into strongly correlated magnetic materials dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates field-induced first-order magnetic transitions and phase coexistence within BaCo2(AsO4)2, a material proposed as a Kitaev quantum spin liquid candidate.

**Main result.** Neutron scattering and transport measurements reveal that the transitions are first-order, characterized by phase coexistence (UUD/FM), rather than continuous evolution toward a QSL state. Theoretical modeling suggests observed excitations arise from bound spin-flip pairs.

**Method.** The research combines inelastic neutron scattering to probe magnetic excitations with transport measurements like the Spin Seebeck effect and theoretical modeling of magnon interactions.

**Model / system.** The system is BaCo2(AsO4)2, an insulating material with Co2+ ions arranged on a honeycomb lattice. It exhibits multiple magnetic phases (double-zigzag, UUD, FM) whose transitions are studied under applied magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Magnetic order parameters (UUD, FM), excitation spectra (spin waves, localized modes), thermal conductivity ($\kappa/T$), and the sign/magnitude of the Spin Seebeck coefficient.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied in-plane magnetic field ($B$) near critical fields like 0.15 T and 0.5 T; temperature ($T$); energy scales (meV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that observed excitations can be modeled by bound spin-flip pairs rather than requiring a gapless spinon Fermi surface characteristic of a true QSL.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic phase diagram, field-dependent excitation spectra showing mode separation and collapse at transitions, and transport measurements (SMR/SSE) confirming hysteresis associated with first-order changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the complex magnetic phase diagram via neutron scattering to detailing evidence of first-order transitions through multiple probes (INS, SMR, SSE), culminating in theoretical modeling that reinterprets anomalous transport signals as bound excitations rather than QSL signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

BaCo2(AsO4)2 (BCAO) is an insulating Kitaev quantum spin liquid candidate with a rich low-temperature phase diagram. Below 5 K, it exhibits double-zigzag magnetic order. Upon application of an in-plane magnetic field, the magnetic structure first transforms into an up-up-down (UUD) state near 0.12 T and then enters a fully spin-polarized ferromagnetic (FM) state near 0.5 T. In the narrow field regime close to the polarized phase, a finite residual thermal conductivity has been reported, suggesting a possible field-induced quantum spin liquid phase. Using neutron scattering, we show that the field-induced double-zigzag-to-UUD transition near 0.15 T is accompanied by the emergence of a cluster of localized UUD spin excitations that coexist with conventional spin waves. Upon further increasing field, BCAO undergoes a first-order UUD-to-FM transition with coexistence of UUD and FM phases, accompanied by a sharp response in the spin Seebeck coefficient. These results do not support a quantum spin liquid scenario near the UUD-to-FM critical field. Instead, modeling indicates that the broad excitations arise from bound spin-flip pairs, while a low-lying dispersive branch near the FM phase boundary carries the same sign of magnetization as the FM order. These excitations naturally account for the observed sign of the spin Seebeck response and are likely relevant to the thermal conductivity near the critical field.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a theory showing that polymer topology dictates the phase behavior of polyelectrolytes, enabling control over coacervation independent of simple chemical changes. By using RPA within a statistical mechanics framework, the authors demonstrate that compact architectures enhance liquid-liquid phase separation propensity. This provides a powerful design principle for charged macromolecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical polyelectrolyte physics, the rigorous application of mean-field approximations (RPA) to complex many-body systems and the development of topology-dependent interaction parameters share conceptual links with modeling correlations in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a topology-specific theory for polyelectrolyte coacervation and understand how polymer architecture influences the phase behavior of charged macromolecules.

**Main result.** Polymer topology acts as a powerful design lever, allowing fine-tuning of phase separation propensity independently of molecular weight or net charge. More compact topologies exhibit a greater tendency for liquid-liquid phase separation (LLPS).

**Method.** The study employs a minimal theory combining the Flory-Huggins free energy with an electrostatic correlation term derived within the Random Phase Approximation (RPA).

**Model / system.** The system consists of polyelectrolytes (charged macromolecules) forming simple or complex coacervates. Topologies studied include linear chains, stars, and dendrimers.

**Key observables.** Propensity for liquid-liquid phase separation (coacervation), effective $\chi$ parameter, charge–charge structure factor $S_{cc}(k)$, and salt partition coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bjerrum length ($\lambda_B$), polymer topology parameters (e.g., number of arms $f$, generations $N_b$), molecular weight ($N$), and salt concentration.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) for electrostatic interactions and assumes Gaussian chain statistics for structure factor derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing coexistence boundaries as a function of $\lambda_B$ or salt concentration, comparing different topologies. Other figures show salt partitioning behavior and scaling laws related to topology.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the free energy functional using RPA, applies it to model various polymer topologies (linear, star, dendrimer), calculates phase diagrams for simple/complex coacervation under varying salt conditions, and analyzes the scaling dependence of effective interactions on molecular weight.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a topology-specific theory of polyelectrolyte coacervation using the random phase approximation and apply it to both simple and complex coacervation. Our results for stars and dendrimers show that more compact chain topologies display a greater propensity for liquid-liquid phase separation, as a function of both Bjerrum length and salt concentration. For mixtures of different topologies, we demonstrate that differences in polymer topology alone are sufficient to drive multiphase coacervation of polyelectrolytes, which we rationalize in terms of an effective $χ$ parameter. Analysis of a simplified global phase diagram reveals that the propensity for such topology-driven phase separation is largest at a finite molecular weight. Overall, our results establish polymer topology as a powerful design lever for tuning the phase diagram of charged macromolecules independently of molecular weight, net charge, and monomer chemistry, since changes in topology enable fine-tuning of the effective charge density without altering these molecular characteristics.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers demonstrated strong intervalley mixing between edge channels in a silicon MOSFET operating in the Quantum Hall regime. This finding establishes that the valley degree of freedom acts as an effective 'valley beam splitter' for quantum information processing. The results confirm this capability while simultaneously showing that spin-flip processes are strongly suppressed, making Si a promising platform.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how engineered valley coupling in a solid-state platform can mimic quantum beam-splitter operations, which is highly relevant for building scalable quantum interferometers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates strong intervalley mixing between copropagating quantum Hall edge channels to establish a fundamental building block for compact silicon quantum Hall interferometers.

**Main result.** Strong intervalley mixing was observed with a transition probability near 1/2, while interchannel transport requiring spin-flip scattering showed negligible probability, confirming the utility of valley degrees of freedom in Si MOSFETs.

**Method.** Measurements were performed on a double-layer-gated Si MOSFET in the Quantum Hall regime using low-frequency lock-in techniques to measure conductance and extract transition probabilities via Landauer–Büttiker formalism.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a double-layer-gated Silicon Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor Field-Effect Transistor (Si MOSFET) operating under high magnetic fields in the Quantum Hall regime. The valley degree of freedom is exploited as an alternative to spin for channel manipulation.

**Key observables.** Transition probability ($T_v$) between valley edge channels, and transition probability ($T_s$) for spin-flip interchannel transport.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bulk filling factor ($
u=2$), low temperature (280 mK), high magnetic field (12.92 T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on accurately subtracting parasitic resistances from raw conductance measurements using the Landauer–Büttiker formalism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device setup, two-terminal conductance plateaus confirming filling factors ($
u=2$), and schematics detailing the geometry for measuring intervalley transitions ($T_v$) versus spin-flip transport ($T_s$).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup and measurement techniques using gate voltages to tune edge channel populations, followed by quantitative analysis using Landauer–Büttiker equations to extract the transition probabilities $T_v$ and $T_s$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Copropagating quantum Hall edge channels provide a promising platform for compact electron interferometry and flying quantum states. In silicon, the valley degree of freedom offers a natural alternative to spin-resolved edge channels because spin-flip scattering is strongly suppressed by the weak spin-orbit interaction. Here, we investigate interchannel transitions between copropagating valley edge channels in a double-layer-gated silicon metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistor. With the bulk filling factor set to $ν=2$, two spin-polarized valley edge channels are brought into close proximity near a depleted side gate. We observe strong intervalley mixing, with a transition probability close to 1/2, indicating nearly complete equilibration between the two valley edge channels. In contrast, interchannel transport between edge channels with different spin orientations shows negligible transition probability, consistent with suppressed spin-flip scattering in silicon. These results demonstrate that intervalley coupling at a Si/SiO$_2$ interface can provide a beam-splitter-like operation for copropagating valley edge channels, establishing a key building block toward compact silicon quantum Hall interferometers.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the energy landscapes on sparse random graphs to determine if they possess a universal ultrametric organization. By comparing a kinetically derived metric with the ideal single-linkage ultrametric, the authors prove that this hierarchy emerges robustly when the energy differences are large. This suggests that local tree-like topology dictates the long-term dynamical structure of such complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of energy landscape organization into an ultrametric structure is highly relevant for understanding complex dynamics, relaxation pathways, and metastable states in disordered quantum systems or molecular folding processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the energy landscape organization on sparse random graphs exhibits universal ultrametricity in the limit of large energy spreads.

**Main result.** The kinetic metric between basins converges pointwise to the classical single-linkage ultrametric as the energy spread ($\Delta F$) tends to infinity, suggesting ultrametricity is a universal property for such systems.

**Method.** The authors use spectral decomposition on a transformed rate matrix (Mahalanobis metric) and prove limit theorems comparing this kinetic distance to the minimax/single-linkage distance.

**Model / system.** The system models energy landscapes defined by random free energies ($F_i$) assigned to vertices of sparse Erdős–Rényi graphs. Dynamics are governed by a continuous-time Markov process described by transition rates derived from these free energies.

**Key observables.** Degree of nontrivial ultrametricity, kinetic Mahalanobis metric $D(B_a, B_b)$, and the single-linkage ultrametric $ho(0)(B_a, B_b)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Energy spread ($\Delta F$), Temperature ($T$), Graph connectivity (sparse/locally tree-like).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that for large $\Delta F$, the contribution from suboptimal paths is exponentially suppressed, allowing the kinetic metric to be dominated by the minimax path.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the Markovian dynamics and constructing a computable symmetric metric (Chunk 2), to establishing the mathematical link between this kinetic metric and the single-linkage ultrametric via limit theorems (Chunks 1 & 3), culminating in a discussion of topological origins (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the ultrametric organization of energy landscapes defined on sparse random Erdős--Rényi graphs. Each graph vertex is assigned a random free energy from a uniform distribution over an interval of width $ΔF$, and the kinetics are modeled by a Markov process with Kramers transition rates. Using spectral decomposition of the rate matrix, we construct a kinetic Mahalanobis metric between basins of attraction. Computational experiments for graphs with $V=5000$ vertices and $E=5000$ edges show that the degree of nontrivial ultrametricity increases monotonically from $\approx42\%$ for $ΔF=10$ kJ/mol to $\approx96\%$ for $ΔF=1000$ kJ/mol. We prove a limit theorem: as $ΔF\to\infty$, the logarithmic asymptotics of this metric converge pointwise to the classical single-linkage ultrametric. For finite $ΔF$, corrections from suboptimal paths are exponentially suppressed with increasing $ΔF$, so that the metric becomes asymptotically ultrametric. Our results suggest that ultrametricity is a universal property of sparse, locally tree-like networks with rugged energy landscapes in the limit of large energy spreads.

</details>

### [Classical codes violate the conjectured square-root bound for quantum random access codes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15617v1)

**Authors:** Kangqiao Liu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15617v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.15617_figures/2607.15617_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Counterexamples fill the asymptotic interval between the conjectured and Nayak bounds.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work proves that classical random access codes can violate a conjectured square-root bound governing Quantum Random Access Codes (QRACs). By embedding these classical structures into the QRAC model, the authors construct explicit counterexamples showing the violation holds across an entire open interval of success probabilities. This reveals that the separation stems from the inclusion of classical coding rate limitations within the general quantum framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum information theory, the rigorous violation of an expected bound highlights fundamental limitations in current theoretical models for quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether a conjectured square-root bound for Quantum Random Access Codes (QRACs) holds true.

**Main result.** Classical random access codes with private randomness violate this conjectured quantum bound, demonstrating that the classical coding rate is the source of separation from the theoretical limit.

**Method.** The authors embed known classical RAC constructions into the general QRAC model using specific encoding and measurement assumptions. They then apply established achievability theorems for classical codes to construct explicit counterexamples.

**Model / system.** The study centers on Quantum Random Access Codes (QRACs), which encode $n$ classical bits into $m$ qubits, aiming for a worst-case success probability $p$. The analysis compares the conjectured bound with bounds derived from information theory and classical coding limits.

**Key observables.** Worst-case success probability ($p$), input length ($n$), number of qubits ($m$), and the rate $r = m/n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is made across a full open interval for fixed compression rates $r \in (0, 1)$, showing violations for any $p$ between the conjectured bound and Nayak's bound.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on embedding classical codes via diagonal encoding states and commuting decoding measurements. Results are often established in the asymptotic limit ($n 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates that counterexamples fill an open rate window between the conjectured square-root bound and the information-theoretic Nayak bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the equivalence between certain QRAC statistics and classical RACs with private randomness. It then uses this embedding to construct explicit, asymptotically large counterexamples that violate the conjectured bound across a continuous range of success probabilities $p$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider whether every quantum random access code (QRAC) with density-operator encodings and arbitrary decoding measurements obeys the conjectured bound $p\leq(1+\sqrt{m/n})/2$, where $n$ classical bits are encoded into $m$ qubits and $p$ is the worst-case success probability. We find that classical random access codes with private randomness, which form a subclass of this QRAC model, violate the bound. We embed these classical codes as QRACs with diagonal encoding states and commuting decoding measurements, and construct pure-state realizations with identical decoding statistics. The achievability theorem of Ambainis, Nayak, Ta-Shma, and Vazirani then yields violations for every fixed $p\in(1/2,1)$ at sufficiently large input length. The counterexamples span the full open interval between the conjectured and Nayak bounds at each fixed compression rate. A finite-blocklength analysis further yields order-optimal logarithmic qubit scaling for a recovery bias scaling as $\sqrt{\log_2 n/n}$ with a sufficiently large prefactor. These results identify the classical coding rate as the source of the separation and motivate restricted bounds based on quantitative spectral properties of decoding measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an efficient, three-stage pipeline to solve the complex routing and spectrum allocation problem in large-scale quantum networks using hyperentangled photons. By combining classical optimization tools like Yen's algorithm, APOPT, and CP-SAT, the authors achieve significant improvements in speed and accuracy over previous methods. This workflow offers a practical heuristic for deploying broadband entanglement distribution across existing fiber optic infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the engineering bottleneck of scaling quantum networks by providing a computationally efficient framework for resource management in existing optical infrastructure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently allocating frequency spectrum and routing entanglement links for large-scale, repeater-less quantum networks.

**Main result.** A novel three-stage heuristic pipeline was developed that significantly outperforms prior genetic algorithm methods in speed and accuracy for optimizing broadband entanglement distribution.

**Method.** The approach combines Yen's algorithm for pathfinding, the Advanced Process Optimizer (APOPT) for rate maximization under fidelity constraints, and a CP-SAT solver for contention-free frequency bin assignment.

**Model / system.** The model describes an arbitrary quantum network using hyperentangled photons in a flex-grid WDM setup. Links are characterized by fidelity ($F_\ell$) and coincidence rates ($R_\ell$), which depend on allocated frequencies and optical losses.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F_\ell$), total coincidence rate ($R_\ell$), network utility ($U$), and solution runtime.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Source flux ($\mu_{m\ell}$), optical losses ($\eta_{A\ell}, \eta_{B\ell}$), and the number of available frequency bins ($K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that channel distortion effects are fully compensated, and it focuses on the resource allocation aspects applicable to existing lightwave infrastructure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hardware components (WSSs) and detail the three-stage pipeline: pathfinding (Double Yen), spectrum optimization (APOPT), and frequency scheduling (CP-SAT) applied to example topologies like ring or Manhattan networks.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around defining the NP-hard Quantum Resource Allocation problem, proposing a novel three-stage heuristic pipeline (Route Finding $ightarrow$ Spectrum Allocation $ightarrow$ Bin Assignment), and validating this workflow on representative network topologies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As practical quantum networks approach large-scale deployment, the need for efficient user-to-user frequency allocation is increasing, yet current approaches only provide partial solutions to the routing and spectrum allocation problem for an arbitrary quantum network. We address this challenge for repeater-less flex-grid quantum networks based on hyperentangled photons using an efficient three-stage pipeline combining leading tools in classical networking with recent advances in numerical optimization. First, double instantiations of Yen's algorithm obtain low-loss route candidates between each pair of users and the entanglement sources. Second, the advanced process optimizer (APOPT) obtains frequency channel allocations that maximize distribution rates under fidelity constraints. Finally, the constraint programming solver using satisfiability methods (CP-SAT) assigns specific frequency bins to each link, ensuring that there is no contention between frequencies from different sources. We numerically demonstrate this approach on a representative ring network and a Manhattan incumbent local exchange carrier topology, realizing significant improvements over prior genetic algorithm approaches in speed, accuracy, and scalability. Overall, this pipeline provides an efficient heuristic workflow for optimizing broadband entanglement distribution, applicable to arbitrarily connected quantum networks integrated within the existing lightwave infrastructure.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work applies the Quartet BCS framework to model correlations in neutron-rich Tellurium isotopes. It demonstrates that increasing valence neutrons significantly enhances quartet admixtures and alters proton orbital occupation by shifting weight from conventional pair configurations to higher-order quartet terms. This provides a microscopic understanding of enhanced superfluidity in exotic nuclei.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit inclusion of four-body (quartet) pairing amplitudes provides a mechanism to enhance correlation effects beyond standard BCS theory, offering insights into exotic nuclear superfluidity analogous to pairing in ultracold Fermi gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quartet correlations develop in neutron-rich Tellurium isotopes, extending beyond standard BCS pairing descriptions.

**Main result.** Increasing the number of valence neutrons enhances the quartet admixture in the correlated state and causes a redistribution of proton weight from pair-like to quartet configurations.

**Method.** The authors solve variational equations derived from the Quartet Bardeen-Cooper-Schrieffer (qBCS) theory, treating four-body pairing amplitudes explicitly.

**Model / system.** The system models valence protons and neutrons in neutron-rich Te isotopes, using a ${}^{100}	ext{Sn}$ core and considering particles in the $2d_{5/2} \oplus 1g_{7/2}$ model space with an isovector pairing interaction.

**Key observables.** Valence quartet number ($N_Q$), condensed quartet component, proton occupation fraction of the $1g_{7/2}$ orbit ($	ext{P}_{\pi,g}$), and quartet-induced energy gain ($\Delta E_{	ext{Quartet}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis scans the isotopic chain from $^{104}	ext{Te}$ to $^{116}	ext{Te}$, constrained by empirical neutron pairing gaps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes a charge-independent isovector pairing interaction and neglects isoscalar proton-neutron pairing correlations for simplicity in the initial analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the monotonic increase of $N_Q$ and $\Delta E_{	ext{Quartet}}$ with neutron number, and show how the total proton occupation fraction ($	ext{P}_{\pi,g}$) is composed of pair-like and quartet contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the qBCS framework by solving variational equations for pairing amplitudes. It then calculates key observables like $N_Q$ and $\Delta E_{	ext{Quartet}}$ across an isotopic chain to show increasing correlations, followed by a detailed analysis of proton orbital occupation showing weight redistribution.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quartet correlations in neutron-rich Te isotopes are investigated within the quartet Bardeen-Cooper-Schrieffer (BCS) framework. Taking \(^{100}\)Sn as an inert core, we consider two valence protons and valence neutrons occupying the \(2d_{5/2}\oplus1g_{7/2}\) model space, and solve the quartet BCS variational equations with a charge-independent isovector pairing interaction. The effective pairing strength is constrained from empirical neutron pairing gaps in the Te isotopic chain. We find that the valence quartet number increases as the valence neutron number is enlarged from \(N_{\rm val}=2\) to \(14\). The same increasing behavior is also found for the condensed quartet component. The proton occupation of the \(1g_{7/2}\) orbit is strongly enhanced relative to the conventional like-particle BCS reference and is driven close to the degeneracy-weighted limit. These results suggest that additional valence neutrons enhance the quartet admixture in the correlated quartet BCS state, while redistributing the fixed proton weight from pair-like configurations to quartet configurations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced kinetic theory for calculating the shear viscosity of binary mixtures of dipolar hard spheres. By extending Enskog-Thorne theory and incorporating structural corrections, the authors derive an accurate expression that matches molecular dynamics simulations across a range of densities. It provides a robust theoretical tool for understanding transport properties in complex, strongly interacting classical fluids.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the rigorous application of kinetic theory and the handling of long-range, anisotropic interactions (dipoles) in a dense fluid context provide valuable methodological insights applicable to complex quantum many-body systems where simple approximations fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing a kinetic theory for the shear viscosity of dense binary fluid mixtures composed of strongly-interacting dipolar hard spheres.

**Main result.** The derived expression successfully captures the density and composition dependence of the shear viscosity up to packing fractions around 0.3 without requiring mixture-derived fit parameters, showing good agreement with molecular dynamics simulations.

**Method.** The approach extends Enskog-Thorne theory, building upon Pousaneh and de Wijn's work for pure dipolar fluids by incorporating structural corrections derived from cluster expansions and resumming the residual dipolar contributions exponentially.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a binary mixture of Dipolar Hard Spheres (DHS). The interactions include both hard-core repulsion and long-range dipole-dipole forces, making it a complex many-body classical fluid system.

**Key observables.** Shear viscosity ($\eta$), partial pair distribution functions ($\chi_{ij}$), and effective collision cross sections ($\Omega_{	ext{bmDHS}, ij}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Packing fraction ($\xi$), dipolar coupling constants ($\lambda_{ii}$, $\lambda_{jj}$), and particle sizes ($\sigma_i$). The theory is tested in regimes where dipolar energy can be much larger than $k_B T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes momentum transfer is dominated by hard-sphere core collisions, allowing the use of Enskog-Thorne formalism. It relies on approximations like replacing the hard-sphere contribution with BMCSL results and resumming dipolar terms exponentially.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the predicted shear viscosity ($\eta$) for mixtures with equally sized cores and those with differently sized cores across various combinations of dipolar coupling constants, showing good agreement with simulation data up to $\xi \approx 0.3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the framework based on Enskog-Thorne theory for binary mixtures. It then details the necessary structural inputs ($\chi_{ij}$) by correcting cluster expansion results, leading to the final expression for shear viscosity, which is validated against molecular dynamics simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a kinetic theory for the shear viscosity of dense binary fluid mixtures of strongly-interacting dipolar hard spheres. We derive an expression for the pairwise correlations in the binary mixtures that is accurate up to packing fractions around 0.35. The approach is based on Enskog-Thorne theory, and inspired by the theory for dense pure fluids developed by Pousaneh and de Wijn. It relies on effective coupling parameters obtained from the pure fluids combined with mixing rules and a heuristic expression for the collision integral. We compare our results to viscosities obtained numerically from molecular-dynamics simulations of dipolar hard-sphere fluids. Our expression for the shear viscosity of the binary mixtures captures the density and composition dependent behavior of the binary dipolar fluids up to packing fraction of $ξ\lesssim 0.3$ without any mixture-derived fit parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes RIXS spectra of cuprates using the single-ion model, comparing results obtained via conventional global fitting and advanced neural network analysis. While the ML approach successfully validates the model for $	ext{YBa}_2	ext{Cu}_3	ext{O}_{6}$, discrepancies found in $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ suggest that describing high-energy excitations requires incorporating physics beyond a purely local picture.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of ML/NN techniques to interpret complex spectroscopic data from strongly correlated materials like cuprates is highly relevant. The comparison between different fitting methods provides a rigorous test of theoretical models against experimental reality.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to test the applicability of the local single-ion model for describing $dd$ excitations in cuprate superconductors using Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering (RIXS) spectra.

**Main result.** Agreement between machine learning and conventional fitting methods validates the single-ion picture for YBCO, while discrepancies observed in $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ suggest that additional physics beyond a purely local model is necessary.

**Method.** The analysis combines experimental RIXS data fitting (Global Fitting) with advanced machine learning techniques (Convolutional Neural Networks) trained on synthetic spectra derived from the single-ion Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system under study is cuprates ($	ext{YBa}_2	ext{Cu}_3	ext{O}_{6}$ and $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$), focusing on $dd$ excitations within the $	ext{Cu}\ 3d$ orbitals. The theoretical framework employed is the local single-ion model.

**Key observables.** Excited state energies ($E_{xy}$, $E_{3z^2-r^2}$, $E_{xz/yz}$), Full Width at Half Maximum (FWHM), and asymmetry parameters ($\alpha$) derived from RIXS spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on scattering geometry, polarization channels ($\sigma 	o \sigma$, etc.), and the distinction between spin-flip ($sf$) and non-spin-flip ($nsf$) contributions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include assuming negligible superexchange interaction (equal excitation energy for spin-flip/non-spin-flip) and relying on the single-ion model to describe high-energy excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate RIXS spectra measurements under varying scattering geometries ($\phi$ scans, $(\zeta, 0), (\zeta, \zeta)$) comparing experimental data against predictions from both global fitting and neural network models for YBCO and LCO.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical problem using RIXS data on two cuprates. It then develops a robust analysis pipeline incorporating machine learning to analyze these spectra, culminating in comparisons that validate the model in one case (YBCO) while highlighting its limitations in another (LCO).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate $dd$ excitations in Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering spectra of YBa$_2$Cu$_3$O$_6$ and La$_2$CuO$_4$ using the local single-ion model. The data are analyzed by conventional global fitting and by a convolutional neural network trained within the same theoretical framework. For YBa$_2$Cu$_3$O$_6$, the excited state energies obtained with the two methods coincide, leading to the $xy$, $3z^2-r^2$, $xz/yz$ sequence for increasing energy. This result validates the use of machine learning tools for the analysis of RIXS spectra dominated by $dd$ excitations. By contrast, for La$_2$CuO$_4$, the two methods do not converge to a single solution, revealing the limitations of the single-ion model in describing $dd$ excitations in cuprates and pointing to the role of additional contributions beyond a purely local picture in shaping high-energy excitations.

</details>

### [Object-relative ultraviolet weighting of electromagnetic modes and one-loop ultraviolet finiteness in quantum electrodynamics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16096v1)

**Authors:** Christian Rembe  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.16096v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.16096_figures/2607.16096_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

Comparison of the piecewise effective mode weight- ing W(x) with the differentiable approximation Wapp(x; a) with a = 100 for a representative smoothing parameter (top), and comparison of the corresponding weighted kernel f(x) entering the one-loop matching integral (bottom). The ver- tical line at x = 1 marks the crossover scale. The figure shows that the smooth approximation preserves the infrared plateau and the ultraviolet x−3 asymptotics, while modifying the physically relevant kernel only in a narrow region around the crossover.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proposes a novel method to regularize ultraviolet divergences in Quantum Electrodynamics by applying an object-relative weighting factor to internal electromagnetic modes. This weighting, dependent on the mode frequency measured in the interaction's rest frame, successfully makes several key one-loop calculations finite while maintaining Ward identity consistency. It suggests that localization effects can be modeled through such scale-dependent modifications of vacuum contributions.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on QED renormalization, the concept of introducing an 'effective' scale dependence into field theory calculations to tame UV divergences echoes themes in effective field theories relevant to AMO physics when considering short-range interactions or high-energy cutoffs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates whether localized electromagnetic interactions can be modeled by introducing an effective, object-relative ultraviolet weighting of internal quantum electrodynamics (QED) modes.

**Main result.** Applying this specific weighting successfully renders selected one-loop QED contributions UV finite while preserving a restricted form of the Ward identity. The results suggest that different physical observables may probe distinct effective localization scales.

**Method.** The authors propose an ansatz for the weighting factor, $W(k; k_c)$, which depends on the object-relative covariant mode variable $u \cdot k$, and test its consistency by calculating one-loop corrections to key QED processes.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is Quantum Electrodynamics (QED), focusing on how internal electromagnetic modes behave when interactions are assumed to be highly localized relative to a characteristic scale, $l_c$.

**Key observables.** Anomalous magnetic moment, Bethe-type low-energy Lamb shift estimate, and the Casimir effect.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Characteristic localization scale ($l_c$) leading to wave number $k_c$, and the object-relative mode frequency measured in the interaction's rest frame ($u \cdot k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The weighting is introduced as a heuristic ansatz, not derived from an underlying action. The consistency check is restricted to one-loop diagrams where the weighting acts as a common scalar factor.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of modeling localized interactions via mode weighting, develops the object-relative covariant weighting ansatz based on heuristic arguments, proves its consistency with Ward identities at one loop, and validates it through four specific physical test cases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work explores whether localized electromagnetic interactions can be modeled in terms of an effective object-relative ultraviolet weighting of internal modes. The proposal is motivated heuristically by two considerations: a weak-field self-backreaction estimate for sufficiently localized energy-carrying modes and a three-dimensional overlap argument for localized interactions. In the resulting ansatz, the infrared sector remains unchanged up to a characteristic scale the at angular wavenumber $k_c$, while ultraviolet contributions are suppressed asymptotically by a factor of order $k_c^3/k^3$ with the angular wavenumber $k$. Because a crossover based solely on $k^μk_μ$ is not well suited to the intended mode-based interpretation, the weighting is formulated in terms of the object-relative covariant mode variable $u_μ k^μ$ with the four-velocity $u^μ$, i.e. the mode frequency measured in the rest frame of the localized interaction object. Within this restricted framework, selected one-loop QED contributions considered here become ultraviolet finite, and a restricted one-loop Ward-consistency check is preserved when the same scalar weighting is assigned consistently to the same internal photon mode in self-energy and vertex corrections. Four initial test cases are discussed: the anomalous magnetic moment, a Bethe-type low-energy Lamb-shift estimate, the Casimir effect, and a compact ultraviolet one-loop test. In the first three cases, the weighting leads to physically sensible characteristic scales associated with the electron Compton scale, an atomic bound-state scale, and plate distance, respectively. The results suggest that different observables may probe different effective localization scales. Action-level derivation, spectral consistency, and extension beyond one loop remain open problems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes Quantum Elastic Weight Consolidation (QEWC) to combat catastrophic forgetting in Variational Quantum Classifiers. It replaces classical Fisher Information with the measurement-independent Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) to quantify parameter importance geometrically. The results establish QEWC as a robust, physically motivated method for training quantum models on sequential tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses model robustness in quantum machine learning by grounding regularization in fundamental quantum geometric measures like QFI, which is crucial for understanding information flow in quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is mitigating catastrophic forgetting in quantum models trained sequentially on different tasks.

**Main result.** Quantum Elastic Weight Consolidation (QEWC), which uses the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), successfully mitigates forgetting and provides a physically motivated regularization framework superior to classical methods.

**Method.** The authors adapt the EWC framework by replacing the classical Fisher Information with QFI, providing an information-geometric measure of parameter sensitivity for quantum states.

**Model / system.** The study uses Variational Quantum Classifiers (VQCs) implemented via parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) to perform sequential binary classification tasks.

**Key observables.** Catastrophic forgetting, QFI matrix ($ar{F}^{(j)}_{Q}$), and the resulting task-specific loss function $\mathcal{L}_{	ext{EWC}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization strength hyperparameter ($\lambda$) controls the stability-plasticity trade-off.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A key assumption for tractability is the use of a diagonal approximation for the full QFI matrix, which neglects off-diagonal correlations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but comparisons are made between unregularized training, CFI-based EWC, and QFI-based QEWC across sequential tasks.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of quantum continual learning, derives the QEWC objective using QFI, compares it to classical methods (CFI), analyzes its performance under noise, and provides theoretical bounds on state stability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum continual learning aims to train quantum models on sequential tasks without losing previously learned knowledge. However, variational quantum classifiers (VQCs) are prone to catastrophic forgetting under nonstationary task distributions. We propose quantum elastic weight consolidation (QEWC), a quantum Fisher information (QFI)-informed regularization method for mitigating forgetting. Unlike conventional elastic weight consolidation based on classical Fisher information (CFI), which measures parameter importance through measurement-dependent output statistics, QEWC uses QFI to quantify the intrinsic sensitivity of the parameterized quantum state. This gives an information-geometric view in which important parameters are identified by the local response of the quantum state manifold. We evaluate QEWC on VQCs trained on sequential binary classification tasks, including classical image-classification and quantum phase-classification tasks. Simulations show that sequential training without regularization causes severe forgetting, while both CFI-based EWC and QFI-based QEWC improve retention of previous tasks. Mechanistic analyses further show that the two methods impose different regularization geometries: CFI acts selectively on measurement-sensitive directions, whereas QFI imposes a denser state-geometric constraint over parameter space. Under depolarizing noise, CFI values are strongly suppressed by degraded measurement statistics, while QFI preserves a more stable sensitivity structure of the noisy parameterized quantum state. These results establish QEWC as a physically motivated approach for studying and mitigating forgetting in quantum continual learning through quantum-state geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced quantum computational technique to estimate ground-state energies for electronic structure problems. It improves upon existing methods by utilizing Sum-of-Squares Spectral Amplification on a first-quantized Hamiltonian representation. The resulting protocol offers substantial, provable reductions in the required quantum resources for simulating materials and chemical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum algorithm efficiency by providing a provable, significant speedup in simulating fundamental physical systems (chemistry/materials) using first-quantized representations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the asymptotic gate complexity for estimating the ground-state energy of electronic structure Hamiltonians using quantum algorithms.

**Main result.** By employing a Sum-of-Squares Spectral Amplification (SOSSA) protocol based on the total charge density operator, the authors achieve an asymptotic reduction in resource estimates for ab initio simulations by a factor up to $44	imes$.

**Method.** The core method involves reformulating the first-quantized Hamiltonian into a Sum-of-Squares form and applying spectral gap amplification techniques within Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE).

**Model / system.** The system is the electronic structure of chemical or material systems, modeled using a non-relativistic Hamiltonian ($H = T + V + U$) represented in a plane wave basis.

**Key observables.** Gate complexity, Block Encoding Normalization ($\lambda$), and the effective 1-norm ($\lambda_{eff}$), which dictate resource requirements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of electrons ($\eta$), simulation grid spacing ($\Delta$), and the gap energy ($E_{gap}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Born-Oppenheimer approximation is used, and the analysis relies on deriving an analytical SOS representation using the total charge density operator.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the focus is on mathematical scaling improvements ($\lambda$ comparisons) rather than visual figure descriptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework for applying SOSSA to first-quantized Hamiltonians, deriving improved scaling laws for block encoding costs compared to prior LCU methods, and quantifying the resulting resource reduction factor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate an asymptotic gate complexity improvement in first-quantized ground-state energy estimation of electronic structure Hamiltonians in a plane wave basis by employing the sum-of-squares spectral gap amplification protocol. The improvement relies on identifying a sum-of-squares representation of the Hamiltonian which provides a lower bound certificate and low cost block encoding that leads to a provably lower quantum phase estimation gate cost. This is achieved by using a sum-of-squares operator generated by the total charge density operator resulting in a block encoding normalization improvement of $λ= \mathcal{O}\left(ηΔ^{-1.5}+η^{1.5}Δ^{-1} \right)$ compared to prior work $λ= \mathcal{O}(ηΔ^{-2}+η^2Δ^{-1})$ where $η$ is the number of electrons and $Δ$ is the simulation grid spacing. The asymptotic reduction in block encoding normalization and similar block encoding costs to prior work is demonstrated to reduce resource estimates for materials and chemical systems by a factor of $2 - 44\times$ corresponding to the lowest cost estimates for ab initio materials simulation.

</details>
