### [Alleviating the Sparse Matrix Scaling Bottleneck in Adaptive VQE via Greedy Operator Commutativity Partitioning and High-Order Taylor State Evolution](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15906v1)

**Authors:** Hermawan Kresno Dipojono  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15906v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.15906_figures/2607.15906_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Benchmark performance of the GOCP-optimized pipeline. (A) Convergence profile of GOCP-optimized ADAPT-VQE for stretched H2O. (B) Landscape sensitivity analysis showing gradient stagnation under the Bravyi-Kitaev mapping, indicating a decoupling of the operator family from the energy gradient.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel framework combining Greedy Operator Commutativity Partitioning (GOCP) with high-order Taylor series expansion to overcome the exponential scaling bottleneck in simulating quantum circuit evolution for molecular electronic structure calculations. By replacing dense matrix exponentiation with sparse operations, it enables accurate ground-state energy determination for large, strongly correlated systems previously deemed intractable.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial algorithmic breakthrough for quantum chemistry simulations, directly addressing the memory and computational scaling limits that restrict the size of systems solvable by current VQE implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the exponential computational bottleneck encountered when simulating the growth of adaptive quantum circuits (ansatz evolution) in VQE variants for strongly correlated systems.

**Main result.** The proposed framework maintains high numerical fidelity while achieving sub-chemical accuracy in ground-state energy calculations, enabling simulations on operator manifolds exceeding $2.68 	imes 10^8$ elements efficiently.

**Method.** It combines Greedy Operator Commutativity Partitioning (GOCP) to manage the operator space with a deterministic fifth-order Taylor series expansion ($\mathcal{O}(5)$) to approximate unitary evolution using sparse matrix-vector operations instead of explicit matrix exponentiation.

**Model / system.** The study applies these methods to molecular electronic structures, benchmarking on systems like BeH2 and H2O. The underlying physics involves mapping fermionic Hamiltonians ($	ext{H}_{	ext{ferm}}$) onto qubit Pauli strings ($	ext{H}_{	ext{qubit}}$) using JW or BK mappings.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy expectation value ($E_0$), state fidelity (maintained $> 1 - 10^{-6}$), and the ability to handle large operator manifolds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The framework is tested across varying qubit numbers (up to 14 qubits) and molecular geometries, demonstrating scalability with high-dimensional operator spaces.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the $\mathcal{O}(5)$ Taylor expansion provides sufficient accuracy for achieving chemical precision in the state evolution approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows convergence profiles for stretched H2O and gradient stagnation under BK mapping. Table II compares scaling metrics (Hilbert Space Dimension vs. Operator Pool size) for JW/BK mappings across H2O and BeH2. Table III summarizes benchmark energy convergence for multiple systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces GOCP to partition the operator pool, followed by the $\mathcal{O}(5)$ Taylor expansion technique to simulate evolution via sparse SpMV operations. Performance is then evaluated across various molecular benchmarks (BeH2, H2O) using both JW and BK mappings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) and its adaptive variants, such as ADAPT-VQE, are central to the study of strongly correlated quantum systems. However, the classical simulation of the ansatz growth process remains constrained by the exponential scaling of operator space and the associated computational cost of unitary evolution. We introduce the Greedy Operator Commutativity Partitioning (GOCP) framework, an analytical methodology designed to optimize both operator selection and state evolution. By reformulating complex unitary rotations as a chained sequence of fifth-order O(5) Taylor series expansions, GOCP bypasses the need for explicit matrix exponentiation, reducing the computational task to a sequence of sparse matrix-vector operations. We evaluate the performance of this framework across diverse molecular systems, including BeH2 and strongly correlated H2O geometries, utilizing both Jordan-Wigner and Bravyi-Kitaev mappings. Our results demonstrate that the GOCP framework maintains exceptional numerical fidelity-exceeding 1 - 10^-6 in state fidelity-while achieving sub-chemical accuracy in ground-state energy calculations. By enabling the simulation of operator manifolds exceeding 2.68 x 10^8 elements with high efficiency, this approach provides a scalable and rigorous pathway for exploring deep variational circuits in complex quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work evaluates the potential of hybrid quantum-classical algorithms, specifically QAOA variants, to solve the challenging AC Optimal Power Flow Unit Commitment problem. The authors tested qubit-efficient encoding methods on small grids but found no performance improvement over classical random sampling. They conclude that demonstrating a true quantum advantage requires scaling up to much larger, currently intractable system sizes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly applies quantum optimization techniques (QAOA) to a major, highly constrained engineering optimization problem, providing a concrete test case for near-term quantum hardware feasibility in applied physics/engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks hybrid quantum-classical variational algorithms for solving the complex Alternating Current Optimal Power Flow Unit Commitment (AC-OPF-UC) problem.

**Main result.** The qubit-efficient hybrid method did not outperform uniform random sampling on small instances, suggesting that much larger system sizes are needed to demonstrate a potential quantum advantage over classical solvers.

**Method.** Two hybrid approaches were investigated: applying QAOA directly to a discretized formulation (requiring too many qubits) and a qubit-efficient approach encoding only binary variables while classically optimizing continuous power flow variables.

**Model / system.** The system is an electrical power grid modeled by the AC-OPF-UC problem, which involves minimizing cost subject to nonconvex AC power-flow constraints and discrete generator commitment decisions. The optimization is formulated using penalty terms within a Hamiltonian for VQA/QAOA.

**Key observables.** Active power (P), reactive power (Q), node voltages, phase angles, and the total system cost function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (number of generators, 5 to 13 tested); required qubit count for full discretization ($\gg$ current capacity).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that a quantum advantage might exist, but the authors note this requires testing systems much larger than their computational capacity allows.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces AC-OPF-UC as a difficult MINLP problem. It details two candidate hybrid algorithms (direct QAOA vs. qubit-efficient encoding). The core is the benchmarking process comparing these methods against established classical solvers like SCIP and uniform sampling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Alternating Current Optimal Power Flow Unit Commitment (AC-OPF-UC) is a difficult mixed-integer nonlinear optimization problem that combines binary generator commitment decisions with nonconvex continuous AC power-flow constraints. In this work, we investigate whether hybrid quantum-classical variational algorithms can improve the solution of single-period AC-OPF-UC relative to classical approaches. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to directly evaluate quantum or hybrid quantum-classical algorithms for the full AC-OPF-UC problem. We consider two candidate algorithms for improving AC-OPF-UC solution quality relative to purely classical methods on ideal quantum hardware. The first applies QAOA directly to a fully discretized formulation of the problem, with equality and inequality constraints incorporated through penalty terms and slack variables. Although conceptually straightforward, this approach requires a prohibitively large number of qubits even for small instances. The second, qubit-efficient approach encodes only the binary generator status variables on a quantum computer, while optimizing the continuous power-flow variables classically for each sampled bitstring. We benchmark this method on randomly generated AC-OPF-UC instances with 5 to 13 generators and compare it against SCIP, SMAC, and uniform random sampling. Our simulations show that the qubit-efficient hybrid method does not outperform uniform sampling. These results suggest that in order to establish potential advantage of the variational hybrid strategy considered here over the best classical algorithms, if any, much larger system sizes (25+ generators) need to be tested, which is beyond our computational capacity. Alternatively, different approaches, such as quantum versions of branch-and-bound methods, may be more promising.

</details>

### [Causality in Pure Quantum Computation with Quantum Control](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15926v1)

**Authors:** Kengo Hirata, Takeshi Tsukada  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15926v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<sub>Figure 1 Causality violation in naive higher-order quantum control in (∗∗). (Left) The function f acts as a NOT gate controlled by the qubit x. Each fin and fout represents the input and output of f. (Right) Applying f to the control qubit itself creates a closed loop.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated type system for higher-order quantum computation that explicitly incorporates physical causality constraints. By extending linear logic with causal connectives, the authors prove that certain programs leading to unphysical operations are rejected by the type checker. This advances the formal understanding of what constitutes a physically realizable quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools for determining which abstract quantum computational models are physically implementable, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant or causality-respecting quantum hardware protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates causality issues that arise when combining quantum control mechanisms (like conditional branching) with higher-order functions in pure quantum computation.

**Main result.** It proposes a novel typed lambda calculus equipped with an intuitionistic BV logic type system, proving that this structure can successfully restrict the language to only define physically realizable supermaps.

**Method.** The authors use proof theory and categorical semantics, developing a specialized type system ($\lambda qif$) grounded in causal linear logic to enforce physical causality constraints on quantum operations.

**Model / system.** The model is pure quantum computation formalized using supermaps (transformations acting on quantum channels) and extended with higher-order functions and quantum conditional branching ($	ext{qif}$).

**Key observables.** Causality violation, physical realizability of supermaps, the structure of resulting linear maps when causality fails.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The type system relies on extensions of Multiplicative Linear Logic (MLL) and intuitionistic BV logic.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that physically unrealizable processes correspond to violations of causal constraints enforced by the proposed type system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate causality violation in naive higher-order control, basic inference rules for the logical framework ($	ext{sequentIBV}$), and programs mapping to proofs.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the problem (causality failure with $	ext{qif}$) to proposing a solution via a type system based on causal logic, culminating in formal proofs within categorical semantics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Indefinite causal order is a characteristic phenomenon in quantum computation, with examples including the quantum SWITCH and the OCB process. Not all such processes are believed to be physically realizable: while some implementations of the quantum SWITCH have been proposed, the OCB process is suspected to be unrealizable. This difference in realizability is commonly attributed to constraints imposed by physical causality.   This paper studies such a causality issue in a higher-order setting, proposing a typed lambda calculus with quantum control and its categorical semantics. Our calculus extends pure quantum computation with higher-order functions and quantum conditional branching, and it is equipped with a type system based on intuitionistic BV logic to enforce causality. We also present a novel model that is closely related to the Caus construction, by which we prove that some physically-unrealizable processes are not definable in our language.

</details>

### [From hyperplanes to hyperellipsoids: characterizing the inherent interpretability of linear and single-qubit mixed-state binary classification models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15433v1)

**Authors:** Kaitlin Gili  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15433v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.15433_figures/2607.15433_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper compares the geometric and interpretability differences between standard linear classifiers and a quantum model based on single-qubit mixed states for binary classification. It shows that the quantum model restricts the decision boundary to an ellipsoid rather than a simple hyperplane. This offers an accessible pedagogical bridge for introducing quantum machine learning concepts using familiar classical ML tools.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, low-dimensional analogy showing how physical constraints (like those imposed by single-qubit measurements) translate into specific structural limitations (ellipsoidal boundaries) on machine learning models, which is relevant to understanding model capacity in quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes and compares the inherent interpretability of standard linear models versus single-qubit mixed-state models for supervised binary classification.

**Main result.** A single-qubit mixed state model results in a decision boundary shaped like a hyperellipsoid, which is mathematically an 'ellipsoid version' of the linear hyperplane boundary.

**Method.** The comparison uses standard supervised binary classification setups, analyzing the geometric constraints and feature importance derived from both the linear weight vector and the probabilistic semi-axes of the ellipsoid.

**Model / system.** The models are a standard linear classifier ($f_w(\mathbf{x})$) and a single qubit mixed state model ($	ext{Tr}[ho_{	ext{mixed}} O]$). The latter constrains the decision boundary to an hyperellipsoid $\sum x_d^2 / a_d^2 = 1$.

**Key observables.** Feature importance (derived from weights $|w_d|$ vs. probabilities $p_d$), and the shape of the decision boundary (hyperplane vs. hyperellipsoid).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative feature importances, represented by the probabilities $p_d$, which determine the semi-axis lengths $a_d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is kept accessible to readers with no quantum background and assumes a standard supervised binary classification setup.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference between the two models using toy data, showing the linear model learning a line and the single-qubit mixed state model learning an ellipsoid in a low-dimensional setting.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces both models, mathematically derives the hyperellipsoidal form for the quantum case, compares their geometric inductive biases (hyperplane vs. hyperellipsoid), analyzes feature importance differences, and concludes with pedagogical suggestions for teaching Quantum ML.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize and compare the inherent interpretability offerings of a standard linear model with a single qubit mixed state model for the task of supervised binary classification. A side by side comparison reveals that a single qubit mixed state model for binary classification is just the ``ellipsoid version" of standard linear model classification. More precisely, rather than learning a hyperplane to classify data, we learn a hyperellipsoid. We discuss the consequences of the geometric inductive biases of both models, as well as how each model contains a different feature importance inductive bias. This short characterization offers an accessible route to quantum machine learning (ML) ideas for readers who have zero background in quantum and are only familiar with linear classification in ML. In support of ML pedagogy, we encourage instructors to utilize this piece to smoothly introduce quantum ML ideas into the undergraduate ML classroom.

</details>

### [Parameterized Quantum Circuit Semantics Through Enriched Categories](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16114v1)

**Authors:** Neil J. Ross, Scott Wesley  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.16114v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced semantic framework using enriched category theory to model parameterized quantum circuits beyond what standard monoidal categories can handle. By enriching the circuit semantics over a parameter category, it successfully incorporates continuous or weighted dependencies inherent in modern quantum machine learning models. This provides a powerful mathematical tool for analyzing complex quantum control structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly abstract mathematical language (enriched categories) to rigorously analyze the structure of parameterized quantum circuits, which is crucial for understanding variational algorithms and QML models where parameter dependence is key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for a semantic model capable of representing parameterized families of quantum circuits, which standard monoidal category approaches fail to capture.

**Main result.** It introduces an enriched category theory framework that successfully models parameterized circuit semantics, providing insights into controlled operations and unifying different perspectives on quantum control.

**Method.** The approach uses enriched category theory, enriching the semantic domain over a parameter category V to encode the necessary structure for continuous or parameterized composition rules.

**Model / system.** The model concerns general quantum circuits, particularly those arising in variational quantum algorithms and Quantum Machine Learning (QML) that involve free parameters (e.g., Pauli-Z rotations $RZ_{p_1} \otimes RZ_{p_2}$).

**Key observables.** Circuit transformations, controlled operations, and the structure of parameterized unitaries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The continuous or discrete nature of the parameters (weights) governing the circuit gates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that standard models fail due to insufficient handling of parameter dependence, necessitating enrichment over a suitable base category V.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the structure of a Frobenius algebra with its components; Figure 2 shows an example of a two-layer real amplitude ansatz circuit parameterized by weights.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from standard monoidal categories to address parameterization limitations, introducing enriched category theory. It studies special cases like Cartesian and monoidal closed parameters, culminating in showing how this framework unifies quantum control perspectives via dagger structures and control functors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is well-known that combinatorial circuits are modeled mathematically by string diagrams in monoidal categories. Given a gate set $Σ$, the circuits over $Σ$ can be thought of as string diagrams in the free monoidal category generated by $Σ$. In this model, circuit semantics are then given by monoidal functors out of this free category. For quantum circuits, this functor is often valued in the category of unitary matrices. This model suffices for concrete quantum circuits, but fails to describe parameterized families of quantum circuits, such as those which arise in the analysis of ansatz circuits. In this paper, we introduce an approach to parameterized circuit semantics, which is based on enriched category theory. We first introduce an abstract categorical construction, and use this to gain new insights on controlled operations and quantum communication. We then study the special cases of Cartesian monoidal parameters and monoidal closed parameters, both endowing the parameterized semantics with useful constructions.We conclude by showing that the monoidal closed case can be used to unify two perspectives on quantum control.

</details>

### [Performance Model for Hybrid Quantum-Classical Workflows](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15426v1)

**Authors:** Pooja Rao, Dimitar Trenev, Jerome Gonthier, Taylor Patti, Sebastian Stern, Tyler Takeshita, Yuri Alexeev, Cedric Lin, Sam McArdle, Justin Lietz, Katherine Klymko, Ermal Rrapaj, Norm Tubman, Krysta Svore, Peter Komar, Elica Kyoseva  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.15426v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

The standard workflow (solid lines) performs a single quantum sampling phase followed by classical subspace construction and diagonalization. The closed-loop variant (dashed lines) iteratively refines the cir- cuit ansatz based on diagonalization results. In both cases, the communication frequency is F = 1 per iteration, yield- ing a low communication-to-computation ratio (Rcc ≪1).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive runtime model to analyze hybrid quantum-classical workflows, separating performance concerns into abstract application levels and strict real-time physical constraints. It demonstrates that while communication overhead can be quantified by a ratio, achieving fault tolerance requires careful consideration of timing feasibility dictated by the underlying hardware coherence times.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides essential architectural guidelines by quantifying when hardware co-location benefits are negligible versus when tight integration is mandatory for feasibility, which guides the roadmap for quantum hardware development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The field lacks a shared framework for analyzing performance when integrating quantum processors with classical High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources in hybrid workflows.

**Main result.** A runtime model decomposes workflow execution into quantum, classical, and communication costs, allowing quantification of whether the bottleneck is computation or communication at different operational regimes.

**Method.** The authors introduce a comprehensive runtime model that analyzes performance at both an abstract application level (using communication-to-computation ratios) and a physical real-time level (using feasibility constraints).

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the hybrid quantum-classical workflow, decomposed into $	ext{T}_{	ext{cycle}} = 	ext{T}_{	ext{C}} + 	ext{T}_{	ext{Q}} + 	ext{T}_{	ext{comm}}$. The analysis distinguishes between application-level timing (logical qubits) and real-time constraints (physical coherence times).

**Key observables.** Communication-to-computation ratio ($	ext{R}_{	ext{cc}}$), Real-time feasibility constraint, Logical error probability ($P_L(r)$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** Classical/Quantum Costs ($	ext{CC}, 	ext{CQ}$), Communication Latency ($L$), Bandwidth ($B$), Physical coherence time ($	au_{	ext{phys}}$), Code distance ($d$)

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes distinct levels of analysis (application vs. real-time) and provides specific formulas for decomposing cycle time components.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the decomposition of total cycle time; Figure D2 shows how logical error probability increases with syndrome extraction cycles ($r$) for different code distances ($d$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for a shared framework, then introduces the runtime model decomposing costs into three components. It analyzes performance across application and real-time regimes, applies this to various algorithms (like SQSP-MaF), and finally discusses error accumulation in fault tolerance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid quantum-classical workflows are expected to underpin practical quantum computing applications, yet the quantum and HPC communities lack a shared framework for reasoning about where and when their integration requirements matter most. Such a framework must separate two distinct levels of analysis: the application level, where communication overhead affects runtime performance, and the real-time level, where it determines feasibility. To address this, we introduce a runtime model that decomposes workflow execution into quantum compute, classical compute, and communication costs. At the application level, a communication-to-computation ratio from this decomposition quantifies whether a workflow is communication-bound or compute-bound; at the real-time level, a feasibility constraint determines whether timing requirements can be met at all, with the reaction time setting the logical clock speed of fault-tolerant computation once they are. Application of this model to representative workflows demonstrates that co-location of quantum processors with HPC infrastructure offers negligible performance benefit for compute-intensive applications today, while tight integration remains crucial for real-time tasks such as quantum error correction needed for large scale quantum computations. However, we discuss how even at the application level these assessments may shift with hardware evolution, illustrating how the model can identify specific crossover conditions, and how, under fault tolerance, the reaction time can set application-level performance.

</details>

**Authors:** Javier Mancilla, Tomás Tagliani

But the very cleanness is the tell: one oscillating coordinate means an enumerable spectrum, and a supervised search finds u in seconds, so C5 assigns this substrate to the order-matched classical (Section 6.2). The real target’s mass (green) sits at low frequency, where every classical model operates com- fortably. The certified dense-spectrum substrate has no such single peak to find.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper argues that current QML failures on public datasets stem from the inherent structure of the data, which rarely meets strict mathematical criteria for quantum advantage. The authors introduce SPECTRA, a two-tier certificate to test for these rare structures, demonstrating that true potential lies in analyzing signals with high-order, non-separable spectral interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantifiable framework—a 'recipe'—to distinguish genuine quantum computational advantages from mere model tuning or dataset artifacts in the context of structured physical data analysis.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models consistently underperform classical baselines on public tabular datasets, leading to skepticism about quantum advantage in ML.

**Main result.** A genuine quantum advantage is structurally limited to highly specific data regimes characterized by high-order, off-grid spectral interactions. The authors propose a 'Fourier Wall' certificate to identify these rare candidates.

**Method.** They introduce SPECTRA, a two-tier certification process: a structural screen checking five necessary conditions (e.g., non-separable order $\ge 3$) followed by a decisive comparison against a complete set of tuned classical benchmarks using paired-bootstrap confidence bounds.

**Model / system.** The core model is an angle-encoded variational quantum network (QNN) implementing a partial Fourier series, tested against various classical ML models like Gradient-boosted trees and Joint Additive Models on structured data.

**Key observables.** ROC-AUC scores, the confidence bound of performance gap ($\Delta$), and the exponential scaling cost of exact classical simulation vs. quantum circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction order $\ge 3$, off-grid spectrum ratio ($ho_{	ext{off}}$), system size ($n \approx 13-40$ sites), and the crossover point where classical simulation becomes intractable.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The advantage is structural, requiring data to possess a hidden structure of jointly interacting periodic rhythms; failure to include an order-matched classical twin invalidates the claim.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SPECTRA pipeline (Tier 1 gates), performance gaps in controlled energy load tests (showing QNN beating classical when conditions are met), and resource scaling showing exponential classical cost growth versus flat quantum budget.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of null results, introduces the structural criteria for advantage (the five necessary conditions), details the two-tier SPECTRA certificate methodology, validates it on controlled synthetic data demonstrating success, and concludes with a discussion of resource scaling and deployment recipes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Across public tabular benchmarks, quantum machine-learning (QML) models usually lose to carefully tuned classical baselines. We argue that this is a structural property of the datasets rather than merely a limitation of current models. Because an angle-encoded quantum neural network is a partial Fourier series, a genuine advantage can arise only when the target spectrum is simultaneously off-grid, of interaction order at least three, high-frequency, supported by near-independent features, and dense beyond practical enumeration. We call the failure to satisfy these conditions the Fourier wall. We operationalize the conditions as SPECTRA, a two-tier certificate: a simulator-free structural screen followed by a decisive comparison between a matched quantum model and five tuned classical twins using paired-bootstrap confidence bounds. On industrial smart-meter data, SPECTRA correctly refuses the real peak-load target, for which gradient-boosted trees reach a held-out ROC-AUC of 0.999. On the same real energy phases with labels generated by an interacting quantum process, the dynamics-matched quantum model reaches 0.994 versus 0.699 for the best generic classical baseline, and collapses to chance when its interaction couplings are ablated. An exact classical simulator ties the quantum model at small width but incurs measured exponential evaluation cost, with an estimated hardware crossover near 13-19 sites. These results provide a practical recipe for identifying, engineering, and deploying quantum-advantage candidates in tabular data.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates spatial correlations in a periodically driven transverse field Ising model. By analyzing the pole structure of the correlation function, the authors show that tuning the drive period allows for constructive interference between different excitation sources (Kibble-Zurek and Floquet resonances). This interference leads to an unusually slow, algebraic decay of correlations, suggesting control over quantum coherence in driven many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in periodically driven quantum systems, which is highly relevant to understanding Floquet engineering and how external driving can manipulate correlation decay rates beyond equilibrium predictions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the long-time spatial correlations of excitations within a periodically driven transverse field Ising model.

**Main result.** The correlation length can be made arbitrarily large by tuning the drive period, due to constructive interference between contributions from the Kibble-Zurek mechanism and Floquet resonances, leading to an optimal algebraic decay rate of r^-2.

**Method.** The analysis uses Jordan-Wigner transformations to map spins to fermions, followed by analyzing the pole structure of the correlation function in momentum space near the unit circle.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional Transverse Field Ising Model (TFIM) subjected to a periodic drive $g(t)$. The Hamiltonian involves nearest-neighbor interactions and a time-dependent transverse field term.

**Key observables.** Longest correlation length ($\xi$) and the spatial correlation function $ho(r, t)$ for excitations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving period ($T$), which must satisfy specific conditions related to Floquet resonances; the coupling constants $g_0$ and $g_1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on identifying poles in the correlation function integral, specifically requiring the interference between two distinct pole contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates numerical calculations of $|ho(r)|$ for varying drive amplitudes. Figure 3 compares the analytically derived optimal envelope $|ho^*(r)|$ with its calculated form at specific resonance conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the system's dynamics by transforming it into a fermionic problem, deriving the correlation function from four-fermion expectation values, and then performing a detailed pole analysis to identify the mechanism responsible for long-range correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a periodically driven transverse field Ising model and study the long-time behavior of the correlations between the excitations in the spin chain. We show that the longest correlation length is defined by the interference between the topological defects induced by the analog of the Kibble-Zurek mechanism and those induced by the Floquet resonance. We show that although the correlations always have a finite correlation length since the system is integrable, the correlation length can be made arbitrarily large by tuning the drive period.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates how quantum effects modify friction in confined water flows by modeling electron interactions using the Wigner equation. Numerical simulations reveal a signature of negative quantum friction, which substantially reduces the drag force experienced by the liquid. This work establishes a theoretical bridge between continuum kinetic theories and particle dynamics for nanoscale energy transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics where quantum corrections significantly alter macroscopic transport properties (friction), offering insights into energy conversion at the nanoscale boundary between classical and quantum regimes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the phenomenon of 'quantum' friction occurring during nanoscale water flows, specifically examining how quantum fluctuations modify energy dissipation.

**Main result.** Negative quantum friction is predicted and observed, leading to a significant reduction (up to 40%) in the frictional drag experienced by the flowing water compared to classical predictions.

**Method.** The research employs numerical simulations solving a fractional Langevin equation derived from modeling electron dynamics using the Wigner picture.

**Model / system.** The system models electrons moving in a solid wall confining nanoscale water flows, where interactions are mediated by charge fluctuations ('hydrons') in the liquid. The theory bridges continuum kinetic descriptions with particle-based simulations.

**Key observables.** Negative quantum friction (net momentum transfer from electrons to water), average current $\langle ar{J} angle$, and force profiles ($F_{1/3}(x)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantumness parameter ($q$), fluctuation amplitude ($W$), and the degree of coupling between classical and quantum fluctuations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes the Wigner formulation as an alternative to Keldysh formalism, and approximations include quasi-static updates for the potential $V(x)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of current $J$ showing decay with increasing quantum parameters, quantifying the reduction in average current $\langle ar{J} angle$, and displaying probability density functions of the fractional force components.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds a theoretical framework using the Wigner equation to model electron-water interaction. It then uses numerical simulations on a fractional Langevin equation to calculate observables, demonstrating how quantum effects manifest as negative friction coefficients that reduce drag.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore the phenomenon of "quantum" friction based on a single-particle model patterned after the Wigner equation describing electrons flow in a solid wall confining nanoscale water flows. The numerical simulations show a clear signature of negative quantum friction, namely a net momentum transfer from the electrons in the solid wall to the flowing water molecules. Such net momentum transfer results into a sizeable reduction of the water friction, up to forty percent, depending on the strength of the coupling between classical and quantum fluctuations. Our results offer the prospect of a theoretical framework bridging classical and quantum description by using continuum kinetic theories and particle-based simulations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper uses Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory to study pattern formation in a cross-diffusion model for plant growth under toxicity. By analyzing the geometry of the system's critical manifold, the authors determine necessary conditions for the existence of stable front-type patterns based on the specific functional forms governing the reaction kinetics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ecology/biology, the mathematical machinery—GSPT applied to reaction-diffusion systems exhibiting far-from-equilibrium dynamics and complex geometric structures like heteroclinic connections—is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics models in physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to construct and prove the existence of stationary, periodic, front-type far-from-equilibrium patterns in a cross-diffusion model describing plant growth influenced by autotoxicity.

**Main result.** Front-type patterns can exist for certain functional forms (like power functions) but are shown not to exist for others (like Holling-type II functions) within the general model class analyzed.

**Method.** The primary tool is Geometric Singular Perturbation Theory (GSPT), which allows extending 1D pattern analysis techniques to include cross-diffusion terms by analyzing the fast-reaction limit and the geometry of the critical manifold.

**Model / system.** The system is a cross-diffusion vegetation-autotoxicity model governed by coupled PDEs involving biomass (R) and toxicity (T). The analysis focuses on reducing this PDE system to an autonomous ODE layer problem using the small parameter $\epsilon$ approximation.

**Key observables.** Pattern formation profiles (fronts, pulses), the geometry of the critical manifold ($C_0$), and the existence/stability of heteroclinic connections between different segments of $C_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cross-diffusion coefficient ($\alpha$), diffusion coefficients ($d_R, d_T$), growth rates, and parameters defining the reaction terms (e.g., $ho$ in the layer problem).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fast-reaction limit approximation ($\epsilon \ll 1$) and makes structural assumptions about the monotonicity of the transition rate functions $f(T)$ and $g(T)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the critical manifold $C_0$ in various parameter spaces (e.g., $(T, P)$-space) and show numerical profiles confirming where pattern existence conditions are met or violated.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the physical model and its general mathematical framework using GSPT. It then analyzes specific simplified cases to derive the layer problem, focusing on characterizing the critical manifold structure through algebraic analysis before concluding with functional form comparisons (power vs. Holling-type II) to determine pattern existence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using geometric singular perturbation theory, we construct stationary, periodic, front-type far-from-equilibrium patterns in a cross-diffusion model for plant growth under the influence of toxicity. We show how existing techniques for the analysis of far-from-equilibrium patterns in one spatial dimension can be extended to include cross-diffusion terms and prove the existence of a one-parameter family of these patterns. For a general cross-diffusion model class, we show when front-type patterns may appear depending on the shape of the critical manifold, which is determined by the specific properties of the reaction terms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes structural superlubricity by examining the role of pressure distribution during sliding contact. It demonstrates that nonuniform pressure, which is more realistic than idealized uniform loading, actually promotes lower friction. The findings suggest that gently curved interfaces with specific elastic properties can achieve highly efficient energy dissipation mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical friction, the analysis of depinning transitions (Aubry transition) and size-dependent critical behavior touches upon concepts relevant to driven many-body systems and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how nonuniform pressure distributions affect structural superlubricity, a state of near-zero friction between crystalline surfaces.

**Main result.** Contrary to expectations, the presence of nonuniform pressure profiles actually enhances structural superlubricity compared to uniform loading conditions.

**Method.** The research employs both analytical calculations for rigid contacts and molecular-dynamics simulations incorporating elastic effects.

**Model / system.** The system models sliding contact between two crystalline surfaces (commensurate or incommensurate) under applied load, using an elastic chain slider interacting with a substrate.

**Key observables.** Average friction per particle, scaling exponent of friction with contact size ($n$), and the transition behavior near the Aubry point.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure profile (uniform vs. Hertzian/nonuniform), incommensurability, slider elastic constant ($k_b$), and applied load.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Early calculations assume rigid contacts; later simulations simplify to a 2D chain on a 1D surface for computational tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare friction vs. load/size dependence under uniform versus nonuniform loads, demonstrating lower friction and altered scaling exponents (e.g., $\propto n^{-0.37}$) in the latter case.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analytical models of rigid contacts showing improved scaling with nonuniform pressure, to molecular dynamics simulations incorporating elasticity, culminating in a detailed comparison across various loading regimes and interface types.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structural superlubricity, nearly vanishing friction between two structurally incommensurate crystalline surfaces, is a promising avenue for reducing friction in applications, but requires very specific and well-controlled conditions. One of those conditions is perfectly uniform atomically flat surfaces. Real-world surfaces are generally rough, leading to nonuniform pressure distributions. We investigate the effects of nonuniform pressure distributions on structural superlubricity, using analytical calculations for rigid contacts as a basis, and molecular-dynamics simulations for a simple model to include the crucial effects of elasticity. We show that a key ingredient is the vanishing pressure at the edge of the contact, and that this leads to improved scaling depinning and scaling behaviour, leading to lower friction. We thus show that nonuniform pressure distributions actually help structural superlubricity, rather than hinder it.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers developed and employed a novel silicon-photonic nanocavity sensor capable of monitoring single protein dynamics at sub-microsecond resolution. By measuring minute shifts in the optical resonance frequency upon conformational change, they resolved complex kinetic pathways in ferritin that were previously obscured by noise or ensemble averaging. This work establishes a powerful new tool for understanding fundamental biophysical processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced optical sensing techniques to study molecular dynamics, which is a core area of AMO physics. The use of cavity resonance shifts as a highly sensitive probe for local changes in polarizability mimics quantum coupling measurements, making it relevant to open quantum systems characterization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Observing protein conformational dynamics occurring on microsecond timescales is challenging due to limitations in measurement speed, Brownian motion noise, and the need for label-free techniques.

**Main result.** The authors successfully resolved tens of thousands of transitions in ferritin over sub-microsecond timescales using a novel photonic sensor, revealing hidden kinetic details like switching kinetics and memory effects.

**Method.** They utilized a fibre-integrated silicon-photonic nanocavity to measure the resonance frequency shift caused by protein binding, achieving high temporal resolution while suppressing Brownian motion noise.

**Model / system.** The system is a slot photonic crystal cavity fabricated on silicon, used as a single-molecule sensor. The dynamics are analyzed using Hidden Markov Models (HMM) and potential energy landscape derivations from probability distributions.

**Key observables.** Cavity resonance frequency shift ($\delta\omega$), Power Spectral Density (PSD), double-well potential shape, transition stepping events, and Mean Squared Displacements (MSDs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sub-microsecond resolution, Brownian motion suppression factor of sixty, ionic strength (NaCl concentration) for tuning dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed fluctuations can be mapped to an effective potential energy landscape via the Boltzmann relation; transition path time calculations assume low energy barriers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the photonic crystal structure and setup, show ferritin monolayer formation and signal stabilization, track fast motions revealing a bimodal potential, and demonstrate stepping dynamics tunable by ionic strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical sensor platform (photonic cavity) to demonstrating high-speed measurements on single molecules (ferritin), followed by detailed kinetic analysis using HMMs and PSD fitting to map out the underlying energy landscape and its tunability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microsecond conformational changes underlie many protein functions, but ensemble averaging has been needed to observe them without labels. Single-shot measurements on individual proteins have had insufficient speed, while protein Brownian-motion has obscured signals. Here, we report a fibre-integrated silicon-photonic sensor that overcomes these barriers, resolving protein dynamics at sub-microsecond speeds in continuous single-shot measurements that can extend over minutes. This is achieved by combining far-sub-wavelength optical field confinement with high optical field uniformity that suppresses protein Brownian-motion by a factor of sixty. In single-shot measurements on ferritin molecules, we observe tens of thousands of transitions consistent with conformational fluctuations of the ferritin shell, resolving them over timescales as short as 400 ns. The ability to continuously monitor transitions over long times reveals switching kinetics, memory effects and molecular heterogeneity hidden in ensemble averages. This opens a new path to improved mechanistic understanding of protein function.

</details>

**Summary.** Researchers used magnon transport measurements on a graphene junction hosting a skyrmion Wigner crystal to study its internal dynamics. By monitoring sharp, periodic fluctuations in the magnon signal as the gate voltage was tuned, they provided direct evidence that individual skyrmions are added sequentially to the crystal lattice. This establishes magnons as a powerful real-space probe for correlated, topologically ordered phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental realization of probing collective dynamics in a topologically ordered state (the skyrmion crystal) using quasiparticle excitations (magnons), which is highly relevant to understanding correlated electron behavior beyond simple band theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is probing the internal dynamics and topology of a skyrmion crystal, whose cooperative crystalline order is inherently fragile.

**Main result.** The detection of near-periodic fluctuations in magnon count confirms that individual skyrmions are added sequentially to the quasi-1D array, providing real-space insight into the lattice stiffness reduction during this process.

**Method.** The study uses non-local magnon transport measurements ($	ext{dV}_{	ext{NL},i}/	ext{dI}$) in a nanoscale graphene junction, acting as a sensitive probe for topological spin textures.

**Model / system.** The system is a nanoscale graphene junction hosting a skyrmion Wigner crystal within the Quantum Hall Effect regime. The physics involves interacting electrons forming topologically protected skyrmions stabilized by Coulomb interaction.

**Key observables.** Near-periodic windows of sharp fluctuations in magnon count, and the non-local signal onset above the Zeeman energy ($E_Z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage (controlling filling factor $
u$), bias voltage ($V_{ds}$), and the excess charge ($\epsilon$) driving skyrmion formation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the non-local response is dominated by bulk magnons propagating across the sample, and that the observed fluctuations correspond to discrete single-skyrmion additions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (graphene junction with gates), show the magnon signal onset at $E_Z$ vs. bias voltage, and display $	ext{dV}_{	ext{NL},i}/	ext{dI}$ dependence on gate voltages to highlight the fluctuation regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (graphene QHE junction), details the measurement technique using non-local magnon transport, presents evidence for skyrmion crystallization via voltage tuning, and interprets the resulting periodic fluctuations as single-skyrmion addition events.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although individual skyrmions are topologically protected objects, their cooperative crystalline order is fragile, easily disrupted by thermal fluctuations or other external perturbations. Probing the internal dynamics of such a crystal is both compelling and challenging, as its intricate and delicate spin texture must remain stable during measurement. Here, we engineer a nanoscale graphene junction hosting a skyrmion Wigner crystal, embedded between magnon emitters and detectors. The skyrmion crystal geometry leaves a striking imprint on magnon transport: as the gate voltage is varied, near-periodic windows of sharp fluctuations in magnon count are detected across the entire sample. We develop an interpretation that this results from skyrmions being added one by one to a quasi-one-dimensional array. Each burst of the fluctuations thus corresponds to the entry of an additional skyrmion, during which the lattice stiffness reduces. The impinging magnons induce and act as a probe of non-equilibrium collective dynamics of the crystal. These results establish a real-space probe of topological spin textures in quantum Hall-type insulating ground states via magnon transport and open opportunities to explore correlated, topologically ordered phases in moire and multilayer graphene systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a theoretical study demonstrating how applying a DC current in graphene can induce strong, controllable nonreciprocity in hybrid plasmon-phonon polaritons within a van der Waals heterostructure. By modeling the Doppler shift effect on the material response, the authors predict measurable spectral and spatial asymmetries that enable magnet-free optical isolation for mid-infrared applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it proposes an electrically tunable platform for directional light control—a key requirement for integrated quantum photonic circuits or advanced sensing applications where optical isolation is paramount.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge addressed is achieving optical isolation by breaking symmetry between forward- and backward-propagating light at the nanoscale without relying on bulky magnetic materials.

**Main result.** The authors theoretically demonstrate that applying a DC current (drift) in graphene induces tunable, momentum-dependent nonreciprocity in hybrid plasmon-phonon polaritons within a van der Waals heterostructure. This effect can be quantified by a contrast reaching $\sim 0.3$ at modest drift velocities.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical modeling using a drift-corrected Random Phase Approximation (RPA) framework to calculate the modified dielectric screening function and subsequent polariton dispersion relations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Graphene/$\alpha$-MoO$_3$/SiC heterostructure operating in the mid-infrared. The model incorporates the coupling between graphene plasmons and hyperbolic phonon polaritons, whose hybridization and resulting nonreciprocity are controlled by an applied electric field (gate voltage) and carrier drift.

**Key observables.** Nonreciprocal contrast $C(\mathbf{q}, \omega)$, momentum-dependent spectral weight imbalance ($\Delta L$), and the real-space near-field intensity modulation $C(r)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drift velocity $\mathbf{v}_d$ (set as a fraction of Fermi velocity, e.g., 5% of $v_F$), graphene doping ($E_F$), and the hyperbolic dispersion characteristics of $\alpha$-MoO$_3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis neglects local-field (exchange–correlation) corrections, which are assumed to be negligible at the relevant wavevectors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device geometry, phonon/plasmon polariton dispersion curves showing anticrossing gaps, and crucially, momentum-space maps ($\Delta L$) and real-space intensity contrast maps ($C(r)$) demonstrating directional asymmetry locked to the drift direction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (heterostructure), detailing the theoretical mechanism (drift-induced Doppler shift modifying RPA calculations), quantifying the effect in momentum space (polariton dispersion and contrast $C$), and finally translating this into a measurable, spatially resolved near-field signature via inverse Fourier transform.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving optical isolation requires breaking symmetry between forward- and backward-propagating light, a long-standing challenge at the nanoscale in the absence of magnetic fields. Here we theoretically demonstrate electrically tunable nonreciprocal phonon-plasmon polaritons in a graphene/$α$-MoO$_3$/SiC heterostructure operating in the mid-infrared. A dc current in graphene induces a wavevector-dependent Doppler shift that breaks reciprocity and generates strong directional asymmetry in hybrid plasmon-phonon propagation. In the reciprocal regime, hybridization between graphene plasmons and hyperbolic phonon polaritons in $α$-MoO$_3$, further shaped by the SiC substrate, enables gate-controlled transitions of isofrequency contours, including canalization along orthogonal crystal axes. At drift velocities of 5% of the Fermi velocity, the system exhibits pronounced momentum-dependent nonreciprocity with contrast reaching $\sim$ 0.3, while directions orthogonal to the drift remain unaffected due to symmetry imposed constraints. Real-space calculations confirm that this momentum-space asymmetry translates into directional near-field intensity modulation. These results establish current-biased van der Waals heterostructures as a platform for electrically tunable, magnet-free nonreciprocal nanophotonics in the mid-infrared.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how coupling Majorana zero modes (MZMs) to a quantum dot can enhance the thermoelectric response. By controlling the superconducting phase difference ($	heta$), the system exhibits strong enhancement of the figure of merit ($ZT_{el}$) via quadratic transmission zeros. This establishes phase-tunable control over electronic transport in topological nanostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical demonstration of engineering thermoelectric properties in topological hybrid systems by exploiting quantum interference effects related to superconducting phases and MZMs, which is highly relevant for advanced solid-state quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the interplay between Majorana zero modes (MZMs) and bound states in the continuum (BICs) dictates the electronic thermoelectric response of a complex nanostructure.

**Main result.** Superconducting phase control is shown to be an efficient mechanism, generating a strong enhancement ($ZT_{el} \simeq 0.75$) by creating quadratic transmission zeros, leading to universal low-temperature transport signatures.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Green-function formalism combined with exact linear-response energy integrals and low-temperature Sommerfeld expansions to calculate thermoelectric coefficients.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a crossbar-shaped quantum dot (QD) coupled to two topological-superconductor nanowires hosting MZMs. The Hamiltonian incorporates terms for leads, the QD level, MZM couplings, and hybridization energies.

**Key observables.** Thermoelectric figure of merit ($ZT_{el}$), electrical conductance ($G$), Seebeck coefficient ($S$), electronic thermal conductance ($\kappa_{	ext{el}}$), and the Lorenz ratio ($L/L_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting phase difference ($	heta$), QD detuning ($\epsilon_0$), and nanowire length asymmetry (affecting MZM coupling strengths).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations are limited to electronic thermal conductance ($\kappa_{el}$), excluding phonon heat transport. The wide-band approximation is used for normal lead self-energies.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the device structure and show how spectral functions (LDOS) and transport quantities ($ZT_{el}$) vary as a function of phase difference, QD energy, and chemical potential, highlighting resonance features.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory using Green's functions in the Nambu basis to calculate linear-response currents for charge and heat. It then analyzes how symmetry breaking (via phase control or detuning) converts BICs into transport-active quasi-BICs, leading to quantitative comparisons of $ZT_{el}$ enhancements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how the interplay between Majorana zero modes (MZMs) and bound states in the continuum (BICs) governs the electronic thermoelectric response of a crossbar-shaped quantum dot (QD) coupled to two topological-superconductor nanowires. Using the Green-function formalism, exact linear-response energy integrals, and their low-temperature Sommerfeld expansion, we analyze the spectral and thermoelectric properties of the system. We show that symmetry breaking converts BICs into quasi-BICs, allowing them to contribute to electrical and thermal transport and thereby generate a finite thermoelectric response. While unequal nanowire lengths, reflected in different intra-Majorana coupling strengths, produce only a modest enhancement of $ZT_{el}$, detuning the QD level increases $ZT_{el}$ by approximately one order of magnitude. Superconducting-phase control produces a much stronger enhancement, reaching $ZT_{el} \simeq 0.75$ through a quadratic transmission zero and a pronounced violation of the Wiedemann-Franz law. The low-temperature values $ZT^{max}_{el} \simeq 0.755$ and $\mathscr{L} /\mathscr{L}_{0} = 21/5$ are universal consequences of this quadratic antiresonance. Our results establish phase-tunable thermoelectric signatures of the Majorana-coupled interference structure and identify superconducting-phase control as an efficient means of engineering the electronic response of topological hybrid nanostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how intense THz laser pulses can coherently drive superconducting fluctuations via a mechanism analogous to phonon excitation, termed the displacive Higgs fluctuation. By calculating the nonlinear optical response, the authors demonstrate that this non-resonant coupling significantly enhances measurable signals near the superconducting gap edge in both s-wave and d-wave superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly bridges quantum optics/driven systems with many-body physics by using intense light fields to probe collective, non-equilibrium degrees of freedom (Higgs mode), which is highly relevant for understanding correlated materials dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the nonlinear optical response of superconductors driven by intense terahertz (THz) laser pulses, focusing on mechanisms beyond standard resonant linear response.

**Main result.** The displacive mechanism, involving a non-resonant static displacement of the superconducting order parameter, is shown to enhance the nonlinear first harmonic signal in both s-wave and d-wave superconductors.

**Method.** Combining numerical simulations (using density matrix expansion) with analytical derivations based on diagrammatic methods to calculate nonlinear response functions.

**Model / system.** Superconductors (s-wave and d-wave) modeled using a tight-binding Hamiltonian, incorporating disorder potentials. The analysis distinguishes between BCS quasiparticle contributions and bosonic collective modes like the Higgs mode.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear first harmonic current ($j_{NL}(\Omega)$), phase shift of the FH vs temperature, and time-domain signal decay rate ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz pulse frequency ($\Omega$), superconducting gap magnitude ($\Delta$), disorder strength ($\gamma$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes a monochromatic pulse limit for simplicity; the treatment of disorder uses an 'exact configuration approach' in some parts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the enhancement of $j_{NL}(\Omega)$ at $\Omega=2\Delta$ when Higgs fluctuations are included (BCS+Higgs vs BCS), and compare theoretical predictions for static order-parameter fluctuations ($\delta\Delta(0)$) used in the nonlinear current formula.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need to study non-resonant response, developing the theoretical framework using density matrix expansions up to third order, demonstrating the mechanism via $j_{NL}(\Omega)$, and validating predictions through temperature dependence measurements (s-wave) or discussing limitations in unconventional pairing (d-wave).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Intense phase-stable terahertz (THz) laser pulses can drive collective modes coherently via multi-photon excitation pathways in a manner different than the standard resonant mechanism operative in linear response. Here we show that in superconductors the nonlinear optical response can be enhanced when excited quasiparticles activate a (non-resonant) static displacement of the superconducting order parameter, in full analogy with the displacive excitation of coherent phonons in opaque materials. By combining numerical simulations with analytical results we demonstrate that the displacive mechanism to excite the Higgs mode is operative in both $s$-wave and $d$-wave superconductors. We validate this prediction experimentally by the temperature dependence of the phase of the nonlinear first harmonic in superconducting $s$-wave NbN. We also discuss how the order-parameter relaxation at large times, which can be experimentally accessed via pump-probe protocols is connected to energy-dissipative processes. Our results offer a novel perspective on the ability of intense THz fields to measure, and eventually control, the parametric dependence of the optical response on collective degrees of freedom.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates that proximity coupling to altermagnets can induce a first-order transition into a topological FFLO state in nanowires. This abrupt phase change is predicted to manifest as a sharp sign reversal in the superconducting diode effect, offering a novel transport fingerprint for these exotic quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological quantum phases (characterized by $Z_2$ invariants) and measurable transport properties like diode efficiency provides a concrete, experimentally accessible signature for exotic superconducting states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates realizing a first-order topological Fulde-Ferrell-Larkin-Ovchinnikov (FFLO) transition in superconducting nanowires proximitized to altermagnets.

**Main result.** This first-order transition is shown to be marked by discontinuous jumps in the pairing amplitude and momentum, which simultaneously drives a sharp sign reversal in the superconducting diode efficiency ($\eta$).

**Method.** The analysis employs Ginzburg-Landau theory applied to the free energy functional derived from mean-field calculations of the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-orbit-coupled nanowire coupled to a $d$-wave altermagnetic substrate. The physics is modeled using BdG Hamiltonians and analyzed via minimizing an effective free energy functional $\Omega(\mathbf{q}, \Delta)$.

**Key observables.** Topological invariant ($Z_2$), superconducting diode efficiency ($\eta$), optimal pairing amplitude ($\Delta_{opt}$), and pairing momentum ($\mathbf{q}_{opt}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic coupling strength ($J_A$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations, the use of a phenomenological Ginzburg-Landau functional, and sometimes quadratic approximations for simplicity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the band structure evolution, the calculation of the topological invariant across phases (cFFLO $	o$ tFFLO), and crucially, show how the superconducting diode efficiency ($\eta$) exhibits a sharp sign reversal at the first-order transition boundary.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian for the system, derives the necessary free energy functional via mean-field theory, maps out the phase diagram showing three FFLO states (cFFLO, nFFLO, tFFLO), and finally uses GL analysis to link the first-order nature of the transition to the observable sign reversal in $\eta$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fulde-Ferrell-Larkin-Ovchinnikov (FFLO) state conventionally emerges via a second-order phase transition driven by finite magnetization. Here we show that a spin-orbit-coupled nanowire proximitized to $d$-wave altermagnets -- with zero net magnetization -- can realize topological FFLO states through a first-order transition, marked by a sharp sign-reversing superconducting diode effect. The altermagnetic field generates band-resolved competing pairing channels, giving rise to a double-valley free energy landscape whose global minimum switches discontinuously. It consequently leads to a first-order topological FFLO transition with simultaneous jumps in the Cooper pairing amplitude and finite center-of-mass momentum. Remarkably, this discontinuous topological reconfiguration substantially enhances the diode efficiency and drives a characteristic sharp sign reversal across the transition. The mechanism of such exotic phenomena is captured by Ginzburg--Landau theory. Our results provide a field-free altermagnetic route to topological FFLO states and identify their direct transport fingerprint.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the transition from synchronized coherence to partial incoherence in coupled nonlinear oscillators, using both electrochemical experiments and theoretical models based on Stuart-Landau equations. The key finding is that this transition is mediated by amplitude defects arising from Hopf bifurcations in low-amplitude components. This mechanism proves that simple phase descriptions are insufficient for capturing the full dynamics of frequency dispersion.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how non-amplitude degrees of freedom (i.e., amplitude dynamics) are crucial for describing complex collective behavior in nonlinear oscillators, which has parallels in driven quantum systems or interacting quantum media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates a transition from complete frequency coherence to partial incoherence, demonstrating that this process is mediated by amplitude defects in a way that phase-only models fail to capture.

**Main result.** The transition occurs when low-amplitude oscillators undergo successive Hopf bifurcations, causing an amplitude defect that shifts the mean frequency of one group while another remains locked, leading to partial incoherence.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements (Ellipsomicroscopy on silicon etching) with theoretical analysis using globally coupled heterogeneous Stuart-Landau oscillators and linear stability theory.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by globally coupled heterogeneous Stuart-Landau oscillators, which are used to simulate the nonlinear feedback dynamics observed during electrochemical dissolution of silicon in fluoride electrolytes.

**Key observables.** Amplitude ($A$), Phase ($\phi$), Mean Frequency, Amplitude Modulation Frequency (Mod. Freq.), and the Participation Ratio (PR) derived from eigenvalue analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($K$) and illumination intensity ($I$); the transition is characterized by crossing critical coupling strengths ($K_{HB}$) leading to Hopf bifurcations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical analysis relies on linear stability theory applied in a rotating frame, assuming the existence of a well-defined frequency-locked state $X^*$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show experimental heatmaps tracking amplitude and phase across different illumination intensities; numerical/theoretical figures illustrate transitions from coherence to partial incoherence via Hopf bifurcations and winding number changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the phenomenon experimentally (varying illumination intensity), then modeling it with coupled oscillators, and finally using advanced stability analysis (Hopf bifurcation, PR calculation) to pinpoint the mechanism of amplitude-defect mediated frequency shifts.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase-only models have contributed significantly to the understanding of synchronization; however, they do not account for dynamical scenarios where amplitude dynamics matter. This study identifies an amplitude-mediated transition from complete frequency coherence to partial incoherence, observed both in an electrochemical silicon-etching experiment and within a population of globally coupled heterogeneous Stuart-Landau oscillators. Strong coupling introduces a bimodal-amplitude distribution from which the transition to partial incoherence is triggered by successive secondary Hopf bifurcations in low-amplitude oscillators. When these modulations cause oscillators to experience amplitude defects, the winding number changes, converting the secondary frequency into a new, oscillator-specific mean frequency. This mechanism results in a partially incoherent state, in which one amplitude group maintains frequency locking while another develops a dispersed frequency branch. These findings demonstrate that amplitude defects offer a pathway to incoherence that phase-only models cannot capture.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that applying controlled electrical power can induce a reversible spin-reorientation transition in Co/Fe3GaTe2 heterostructures. By utilizing Joule heating, the magnetic anisotropy of the system can be tuned from out-of-plane to in-plane states at temperatures below the Curie point. This establishes electrothermal control as a viable method for engineering magnetism in next-generation 2D spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the concept of controlling phase transitions (SRT) using localized, non-equilibrium energy input (Joule heating) is relevant to understanding thermal management in quantum devices or engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how electrothermal effects can control the spin-reorientation transition (SRT) in Co/Fe3GaTe2 van der Waals heterostructures.

**Main result.** Joule heating causes a reversible switch from an out-of-plane to an in-plane magnetization state at a temperature below the Curie point, demonstrating electrothermal anisotropy competition for controlling vdW magnetism.

**Method.** The research employs Magneto-Optical Kerr Effect (MOKE) measurements and power-controlled electrical biasing on the heterostructures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Co/Fe3GaTe2 van der Waals magnet heterostructure, where magnetic anisotropy competition between the deposited Co overlayer and the underlying FGaT substrate dictates the magnetization state.

**Key observables.** Spin-reorientation transition temperature (approx. 311 K), hysteresis loop evolution under applied power (80-100 mW window), and field-assisted domain reversal thresholds ($\sim 15$ mW).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control mechanism is electrothermal, involving Joule heating that modifies the magnetic anisotropy energy balance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed electrical response is attributed to an electrothermal origin rather than purely electrostatic effects due to oxide leakage.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail STM images showing surface morphology and Co growth evolution on FGaT, alongside MOKE traces illustrating temperature-dependent magnetic switching behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context of electrical control in vdW magnets, presents measurements demonstrating the temperature-driven SRT via Joule heating, and confirms this tunability using controlled power application.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electrical control of magnetic anisotropy in van der Waals (vdWs) magnets is a key step toward reconfigurable two-dimensional spintronics, yet how a conventional metallic ferromagnet competes with a van der Waals magnet across a direct interface has remained largely unexplored. Here we demonstrate reversible thermal and electrothermal control of a spin-reorientation transition in Co/Fe_3GaTe_2 (FGaT) heterostructures. As Joule heating weakens the FGaT anisotropy, the heterostructure switches from an out-of-plane- to an in-plane-dominated state at a reorientation temperature of approximately 311 K, well below the Curie temperature, consistent with an exchange-mediated anisotropy competition between the Co overlayer and FGaT. An electrically driven device shows a closely matching loop evolution within an 80-100 mW power window, reversibly over five measurement cycles, consistent with an electrothermal origin. In a Co-free FGaT device, Kerr microscopy traces the switching to a power-tunable domain nucleation barrier and demonstrates power-thresholded, field-assisted magnetization reversal at a threshold near 15 mW. These results demonstrate electrothermal anisotropy competition as a route to heat-assisted and device-level control of vdWs magnetism.

</details>
