**Summary.** This work uses a sophisticated phase-field model coupled with DFT to simulate dendrite growth in sodium solid-state batteries. It reveals that grain boundary geometry causes asymmetric stripping, trapping residual Na metal which accelerates future dendrite penetration. The findings provide crucial design rules by linking microstructure and anode chemistry to improve battery safety.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electrochemistry, the rigorous application of multi-scale phase-field modeling, coupling continuum physics (diffusion/potential) with quantum inputs (DFT), showcases advanced computational techniques relevant for complex non-equilibrium systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Dendrite growth during cycling is a critical, poorly understood challenge limiting the safety and energy density of all-solid-state sodium batteries (SSBs).

**Main result.** The study shows that dendrite stripping is intrinsically asymmetric due to grain boundary geometry, leading to residual, kinetically stabilized Na metal that accelerates subsequent dendrite penetration.

**Method.** A phase-field model, informed by Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, was applied to simulate microstructure evolution during electrodeposition cycles.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D half-cell of Sodium (Na) SSBs using polycrystalline $	ext{Na}_3	ext{SbS}_4$ electrolyte. The model couples Allen-Cahn equations for phase evolution with reaction-diffusion and electric potential equations.

**Key observables.** Dendrite penetration depth, isolated Na metal formation size, microstructure evolution ($\xi$, $\phi_g$), and local current/potential distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied voltage, solid-electrolyte microstructure (GB density), anode chemistry ($	ext{Na}$ vs. $	ext{Na}_3	ext{Sb}$ alloy).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes no side reactions occur during plating and stripping; DFT surface electron densities are used to inform the phase-field parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups, charge distributions from DFT analysis on different crystal planes ((100), (110), (111)), and snapshots showing potential/concentration profiles across various SE microstructures during cycling.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, details the coupled phase-field methodology informed by DFT, presents results demonstrating asymmetric stripping leading to memory effects, and concludes with design principles based on microstructure control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dendrite growth during cycling remains a critical challenge for all-solid-state batteries (SSBs), limiting the full realization of their inherent safety and high energy density. In particular, the mechanisms of continuous dendrite penetration during charge-discharge cycling remain poorly understood and are difficult to characterize experimentally. This study applies a phase-field model, informed by density functional theory calculations, to rationalize and visualize the dendrite penetration behaviors during cycling in sodium (Na) SSBs with pure Na or Na-Sb alloy anodes and polycrystalline Na$_3$SbS$_4$ electrolyte. We show that dendrite stripping is intrinsically asymmetric with respect to plating due to grain boundary geometry, leading to the formation of isolated Na metal that persists between cycles. This residual Na metal becomes kinetically stabilized at grain-boundary junctions and is readily reactivated during subsequent plating, thereby accelerating and amplifying dendrite penetration. We further investigate the effects of applied voltage, solid-electrolyte microstructure, and anode chemistry on this phenomenon. These findings establish isolated Na metal as a key contributor for continued dendrite propagation in Na SSBs and provide design principles for stabilizing anode/electrolyte interfaces in Na SSBs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the challenge of isolating the subtle topological Hall effect originating from skyrmions within thin magnetic films where it is masked by a dominant anomalous contribution. By combining magneto-optical imaging with both electrical and thermal transport measurements, the authors successfully quantify the characteristic topological response ($ho_{THE}$), providing strong evidence for topologically protected spin textures in these nanoscale systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the rigorous separation of emergent topological responses from conventional extrinsic effects (like AHE) using multiple complementary physical probes (electrical vs. thermal) is highly relevant to understanding complex many-body dynamics in engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is separating the small topological Hall effect (THE) signal from the much larger, overlapping anomalous Hall effect (AHE) when measuring skyrmion-related transport phenomena.

**Main result.** The authors successfully measured a finite topological Hall resistivity of $249(18)\, 	ext{p}\Omega	ext{ m}$ at room temperature and confirmed the existence of topologically protected skyrmions using multiple independent measurements.

**Method.** A combination of Magneto-optical Kerr microscopy (MOKE) to image magnetization, electrical transport measurements for THE, and thermal transport measurements for the Topological Nernst Effect (TNE) were employed.

**Model / system.** The study investigates single-layer thick films of Ta/CoFeB/MgO that host topologically protected skyrmion spin structures. The analysis models the total measured resistivity ($ho_{Hall}$) and Nernst effect ($N_{xy}$) as sums of Ordinary, Anomalous, and Topological contributions.

**Key observables.** Topological Hall resistivity ($ho_{THE}$), Topological Nernst coefficient ($N_{TNE}$), surface magnetization images (MOKE), and coercive field decay parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature measurements; film thicknesses in the nanometer range; magnetic anisotropy controlling skyrmion stability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The topological contribution can be isolated by modeling the total signal as a sum of components, and that comparison between THE and TNE provides robust confirmation of topology despite domain wall scattering effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate how AHE and THE hysteresis loops can be separated; one figure shows the setup for measuring THE via electron deflection in a Hall cross configuration.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the difficulty of separating THE from AHE, then detailing the use of MOKE to confirm skyrmion presence. It rigorously disentangles contributions using both electrical (Hall) and thermal (Nernst) transport measurements, culminating in a quantitative measurement of $ho_{THE}$ and $N_{TNE}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The topological Hall effect is a valuable tool to indicate the presence of topologically protected magnetic structures. In this work, we present topological Hall effect measurements originating from topologically protected skyrmions in Ta/CoFeB/MgO single-layer thick films with a one nanometer thick magnetic layer. The simultaneous occurrence of the small topological Hall effect and the dominating anomalous Hall effect in this material system makes direct detection challenging as compared to bulk or multilayer systems. In electronic transport measurements, both effects' contributions impact electron trajectories in the same way, overlapping in the measurement signal, and require disentanglement. Magneto-optical Kerr microscopy was used to image the surface magnetization, enabling the separation of the topological Hall effect from other Hall effect contributions. These measurements reveal a topological Hall resistivity of $249(18)\,$\si{\pico\ohm\meter} for Ta/CoFeB/MgO layer stacks at room temperature. Magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) measurements also allow tracking skyrmion formation during external magnetic field sweeps to confirm their occurrence when measuring the topological Hall effect. We verify this outcome by comparing the results with thermal and electrical transport measurements from which we calculate the overall topological quantity that gives rise to the topological Nernst and Hall effect, respectively.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a design for enhancing Third-Harmonic Generation (THG) using a Photonic Crystal cavity embedded in diamond. By engineering the lattice structure to create doubly resonant quasi-bound states at both the fundamental and third harmonic frequencies, they overcome symmetry limitations that typically suppress nonlinear coupling. The resulting device promises highly efficient, monolithic on-chip frequency conversion applicable across multiple wavelengths.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it demonstrates advanced photonics engineering—using photonic band structure control (BICs) in a solid-state platform like diamond—to enhance nonlinear optical processes, which is a key area of quantum optics and cavity QED research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is achieving efficient, monolithic on-chip frequency conversion, specifically Third-Harmonic Generation (THG), within a centrosymmetric material like diamond for quantum and nonlinear photonics.

**Main result.** The authors designed a photonic crystal heterostructure cavity that achieves a high projected THG efficiency ($\sim 0.034 	ext{ W}^{-2}$ at $Q=200,000$), establishing a robust route for on-chip frequency conversion across various wavelengths.

**Method.** The study uses coupled-mode theory and 3D Finite Difference Time Domain (FDTD) simulations to model the THG process in a doubly resonant cavity.

**Model / system.** The system is a diamond Photonic Crystal (PhC) slab with a hexagonal lattice containing triangular air holes. It is engineered as a heterostructure cavity designed to support simultaneous resonance for the Fundamental Harmonic ($\omega$) and the Third Harmonic ($3\omega$).

**Key observables.** Normalized THG efficiency ($\eta$), Quality Factors ($Q_1, Q_3$), and the nonlinear overlap integral ($eta_3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The frequency tripling condition ($3\omega \approx \omega_3$) must be met; high quality factors ($Q \sim 200,000$) are crucial for enhancement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that breaking the in-plane symmetry by using triangular holes is necessary to activate a non-zero nonlinear coupling ($eta_3$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures (showing FH and TH modes), optimization plots of the Figure of Merit ($Q_3|eta_3|^2$), and 3D field distributions for both fundamental and third harmonic fields in the optimized cavity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical framework using coupled-mode theory, detailing the design strategy involving symmetry breaking (circular to triangular holes) to activate nonlinear coupling, optimizing the heterostructure parameters ($n_{core}, n_{trans}, n_{clad}$), and finally presenting high-Q simulation results demonstrating superior THG performance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose and numerically demonstrate doubly resonant third-harmonic generation (THG) in a diamond photonic crystal (PhC) slab, in which the fundamental harmonic (FH) and the third harmonic (TH) modes are simultaneously resonant within the same membrane. A hexagonal-lattice slab with triangular air holes is designed so that a K-point band-edge FH mode and a $Γ$-point quasi-bound-state-in-the-continuum (quasi-BIC) TH mode satisfy the frequency-tripling condition $3ω_1\approxω_3$. Modifying the hole shape from circular to equilateral triangular breaks the in-plane symmetry that otherwise forces the nonlinear coupling to vanish, thereby converting a TH mode with negligible overlap into one with finite while simultaneously reducing the required slab thickness. Guided by a closed-form expression for THG efficiency derived from coupled-mode theory, we design the unit cell and a PhC heterostructure cavity. Three-dimensional simulations of the designed cavity yield a normalized THG efficiency $η=2.7\times10^{-7}~\mathrm{W}^{-2}$ under moderate quality factors, which is projected to reach ~$0.034~\mathrm{W}^{-2}$ at the fabrication-limited quality factor (Q = 200,000). Because the operating wavelength is set by the lattice constant, this design, combined with the ultra-wide transparency window of diamond, can map a single geometry across various fabricable wavelengths, spanning from telecommunication bands to color-center-resonant visible and deep-UV outputs. These results establish a robust route toward efficient, monolithic on-chip frequency conversion in an all-diamond platform for quantum and nonlinear photonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous, numerically intensive solution to the Migdal-Eliashberg equations for phonon-mediated superconductivity within the jellium model. The authors demonstrate that accounting for full momentum and frequency dependence drastically lowers predicted critical temperatures ($T_c < 1$ K). This result cautions against overestimating high-$T_c$ superconductivity using simplified models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on BCS-like pairing in a simple metal, the rigorous application of full Eliashberg theory and the discussion of necessary approximations (RPA, momentum/frequency dependence) are highly relevant methodologies for analyzing strongly coupled quantum systems in AMO physics or condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to accurately determine the critical temperature ($T_c$) for phonon-mediated superconductivity in the jellium model, specifically challenging previous overestimations of high-$T_c$. The authors investigate whether the simple jellium model can support room-temperature superconductivity.

**Main result.** The full Eliashberg theory calculation yields very low critical temperatures ($T_c < 1$ K) for the jellium model, contradicting earlier optimistic claims. This suggests that more complex physics beyond the basic jellium framework is required for high-$T_c$.

**Method.** The study employs a full numerical solution of the Migdal-Eliashberg equations in their momentum and frequency-dependent form, requiring advanced techniques to handle singularities near the Fermi surface.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the jellium background for phonon-mediated superconductivity. The theory relies on solving the self-consistent Eliashberg equations using approximations like Random Phase Approximation (RPA) for screening.

**Key observables.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), anomalous self-energy ($\Sigma_{12}$), quasiparticle residue ($Z$), and the gap function ($\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron density ($n_e$), Chemical Potential ($\mu$), Fermi wavevector ($k_F$), and phonon frequencies ($\omega_{	ext{ph}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The jellium model is used, which employs the Thomas-Fermi approximation for screening and neglects effects like Friedel oscillations. The full ME equations are solved numerically, requiring approximations for momentum and frequency integration.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate characteristic energy scales ($\epsilon_F$, $\omega_s$, $k_F$) vs density; they also show the real part of the normal self-energy ($\Sigma_{11}$) and the temperature dependence of the anomalous self-energy, confirming renormalization effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework (ME equations), then tackling numerical challenges related to momentum/frequency integration, and finally presenting convergence studies leading to a low $T_c$ estimate for the jellium model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a full numerical solution of the Migdal--Eliashberg equations for the jellium model of phonon-mediated superconductivity. We find very low critical temperatures below 1~K, in contrast to earlier claims that the jellium model for hydrogen solids might reach room-temperature superconductivity. Our results suggest that full momentum and frequency dependence of the gap function and normal self-energy should be taken into account for accurate $T_c$ estimates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally and theoretically proves that near-field electromagnetic coupling between objects alters their far-field thermal emission. By measuring the radiative balance of two microspheres across varying separations, the authors quantify a 'dressed emissivity.' This finding establishes a measurable thermal analogue to the Purcell effect, showing that the surrounding photonic environment dictates the emitted radiation spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct, measurable thermal analogue of the Purcell effect, demonstrating how coupling to an electromagnetic environment modifies fundamental radiation properties (emissivity) in nanoscale systems, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and open system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether near-field electromagnetic coupling between two bodies modifies their far-field thermal radiation, moving beyond simply quantifying the enhanced energy exchange between neighbors.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally and theoretically demonstrate that near-field interactions renormalize the far-field thermal emission by inducing a distance-dependent 'dressed emissivity,' establishing a thermal analogue of the Purcell effect.

**Method.** The work combines high-precision differential calorimetry experiments with advanced theoretical modeling using fluctuational electrodynamics (e.g., SCUFF-EM solvers).

**Model / system.** The system consists of two borosilicate microspheres placed in a vacuum chamber, allowing for controlled separation distances spanning from the far field ($\gg \lambda$) down to the near field ($d \sim 100$ nm).

**Key observables.** Asymmetric heat fluxes, non-monotonic temperature responses, and the extracted distance-dependent dressed emissivity ($\Delta\epsilon_{	ext{dress}}(d)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Separation distance ($d$), which is varied from $120 \mu	ext{m}$ down to hundreds of nanometers; the thermal wavelength ($\lambda_{Wien}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decomposing total radiative power into sphere-bath, sphere-sphere, and external bath components. Semi-analytical models are limited when $d$ approaches $\lambda_{Wien}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (dual-probe platform), temperature variations showing asymmetry vs. separation, and comparisons between full-wave simulations (SCUFF-EM) and semi-analytical predictions for heat flux and dressed emissivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem by noting the gap in understanding near-field effects on emitted radiation. It details the experimental platform and measurement techniques to quantify radiative balance, develops theoretical frameworks using fluctuational electrodynamics, and presents results showing spectral renormalization via the dressed emissivity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Radiative heat transfer at subwavelength distances is generally understood as enhanced energy exchange mediated by photon tunnelling between neighboring bodies. While near-field interactions can dramatically increase mutual heat transfer, whether they also modify the thermal radiation emitted by the bodies themselves remains an open question. Here we experimentally show that near-field electromagnetic coupling reshapes far-field thermal emission through a distance-dependent dressed emissivity. Using a dual-probe calorimetric platform, we independently monitor the radiative balance of two borosilicate microspheres over separations ranging from 120 micrometers to a few hundred nanometers, spanning the transition from the far field to the near field. Nanowatt-resolved differential radiometry reveals asymmetric heat fluxes and a non-monotonic response of the hotter sphere, demonstrating that thermal radiation is governed not only by emitter-bath interactions but also by coupling to the surrounding photonic environment. By analyzing the total power exchanged between the coupled system and the external thermal bath, we directly extract a dressed emissivity and show that near-field interactions renormalize the far-field thermal emission of the pair through a redistribution of the electromagnetic modes available to thermal fluctuations. These observations provide direct experimental evidence that thermal emitters are dressed by their electromagnetic environment, establishing a thermal analogue of the Purcell effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores the complex dynamics of magnetic Hopfions under alternating magnetic fields, revealing that the field regime dictates the outcome. Weak fields cause irreversible topological decay, whereas strong fields enable coherent, reversible switching between different topological states. The frequency of the applied field acts as a switch, selecting distinct physical pathways for this controlled topological manipulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates controlled topological switching using external fields, providing a platform analogous to controlling quantum states or phase transitions in engineered magnetic systems, which has implications for spintronics and nonlinear dynamics modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic Hopfions, which are 3D topological solitons, undergo dynamical transitions when subjected to alternating magnetic (AM) fields.

**Main result.** The system exhibits two distinct regimes: an irreversible transition from Hopfion to toron in weak fields, and a reversible, frequency-dependent switching between ferromagnetic and Hopfion states in strong fields.

**Method.** The research employs 3D micromagnetic simulations combined with analytical mode analysis to track the evolution of topological invariants under time-varying magnetic fields.

**Model / system.** The system models magnetization dynamics within a confined nanostructure containing chiral magnetic layers, governed by the Landau–Lifshitz–Gilbert equation incorporating exchange, DMI, anisotropy, and dipolar interactions.

**Key observables.** Hopf invariant (QH), magnetization texture evolution, total energy, and intrinsic mode amplitudes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Field amplitude ($B_0$) and driving frequency ($f$), which act as independent control knobs determining the switching pathway.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are analyzed by solving micromagnetic equations; the distinction between weak-field (resonant) and strong-field (nonresonant/field-locked) responses is key.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic comparisons of irreversible vs. reversible transitions, time evolution of QH under resonant driving showing breakdown, and detailed temporal traces of magnetization and $Q_H$ for both 2 GHz (breathing) and 40 GHz (rotational) switching pathways.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically contrasts the outcomes of weak-field excitation (leading to instability/irreversibility) against strong-field excitation, demonstrating how frequency selects between low-frequency collective motion paths and high-frequency field-locked limit cycles for reversible control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic Hopfions are three-dimensional (3D) topological solitons characterized by a nonzero Hopf invariant and offer a promising platform for 3D spintronics. While their static stabilization has been widely studied, their nonlinear dynamics under alternating magnetic (AM) fields remain largely unexplored. We show, using 3D micromagnetic simulations and analytical mode analysis, that an AM field drives two qualitatively distinct dynamical regimes of a confined magnetic Hopfion. In the weak-field regime, resonant excitation of intrinsic Hopfion modes induces a nonlinear instability and an irreversible topological reconfiguration from a Hopfion to a toron. In contrast, in the strong-field regime, the system undergoes reversible field-locked topological switching at GHz frequencies, with the magnetization periodically alternating between a topologically trivial ferromagnetic configuration and a Hopfion state. The switching pathway is selected by the driving frequency: a 2 GHz field drives a breathing pathway associated with the low-frequency collective response, whereas a 40 GHz field produces a nonresonant rotational pathway governed by strong Zeeman-torque-driven precession and field locking. These results identify field amplitude and frequency as independent control knobs and reveal that reversible Hopfion switching can arise either from nonlinear continuation of low-frequency collective motion or from nonresonant high-frequency field locking.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates a powerful, contactless technique using Terahertz spectroscopy to map the homogeneity of superconducting $	ext{NbTiN}$ thin films across entire wafers. By correlating spatial variations in normal-state resistance ($R_s$) with changes in the superconducting kinetic inductance ($L_k$), the authors provide a crucial pre-fabrication quality control method for quantum computing components.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials characterization rather than fundamental AMO physics, the use of non-contact THz spectroscopy to probe collective quantum phenomena (superconducting condensate) at wafer scale is highly relevant for developing superconducting quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to ensure the homogeneity of superconducting thin films across entire wafers for reliable use in large-scale quantum devices.

**Main result.** THz mapping proves that local variations in normal-state resistance ($R_s$) correlate with deviations in the zero-temperature kinetic inductance ($L_{k0}$), enabling pre-lithography quality control.

**Method.** The study employs contactless Terahertz (THz) spectroscopy, combining room-temperature frequency-domain mapping for $R_s$ and cryogenic time-domain measurements to probe superconducting gaps and kinetic inductance.

**Model / system.** The system involves Niobium Titanium Nitride ($	ext{NbTiN}$) thin films on silicon wafers. Analysis relies on the Drude model for normal state resistance and Mattis-Bardeen theory for extracting the superconducting energy gap ($2\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Sheet resistance ($R_s$), critical temperature ($T_c$), superconducting energy gap ($2\Delta$), and sheet kinetic inductance ($L_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wafer sizes (4-inch, 6-inch), THz frequency range ($\sim 0.1 - 3 	ext{ THz}$), and temperature regimes (room to cryogenic).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The extraction of $R_s$ from raw spectra assumes the scattering rate is much larger than in the THz regime; fitting superconducting properties uses established BCS/Mattis-Bardeen models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show room-temperature maps of sheet resistance deviations ($\delta R_s$) and cryogenic measurements comparing spatial distributions of $2\Delta$ and $L_{k0}$ across different wafer locations.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from establishing the need for homogeneity in quantum technology, detailing the THz mapping methodology (room vs. cryo), presenting results showing correlations between normal-state and superconducting property variations, and concluding on the technique's feasibility as a pre-screening tool.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For large-scale superconducting quantum technology, e.g. quantum computing, the homogeneity of wafer-scale superconducting thin films is vital for consistent performance of the fabricated devices. Terahertz (THz) spectroscopy as a contactless and non-destructive measurement technique is a powerful tool to characterize the superconducting films. In this work, a set of niobium titanium nitride (NbTiN) thin films on 4-inch and 6-inch silicon wafers, grown via plasma-enhanced magnetron sputtering, are investigated via THz spectroscopy: full wafers are mapped at room temperatures and exemplary segments are characterized at cryogenic temperatures. The deviations in observed sheet resistance depend on the used deposition device and the film thickness. While the deviations in superconducting sheet kinetic inductance match those of the normal-state sheet resistance, the critical temperature and energy gap exhibit little variation. This THz mapping technique demonstrates the feasibility of evaluating wafer-scale superconducting thin films before lithography, facilitating preparation of the thin films for reproducible device fabrication.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the pOv qubit, an encoding scheme leveraging valley-orbit coupling in semiconductor quantum dots. It demonstrates that this system offers enhanced coherence by operating at noise-immune 'sweet spots' and provides a novel pathway for implementing tunable two-qubit gates using the quadrupole-quadrupole interaction.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses key challenges in solid-state quantum computing: achieving long coherence times ($T_2^*$) through engineered Hamiltonian symmetries (sweet spots) and enabling scalable, controllable interactions between qubits via electric field tuning.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a robust qubit encoding in semiconductor quantum dots that maintains fast electrical control while suppressing decoherence from charge noise.

**Main result.** A novel pOv qubit utilizing valley-orbit coupling (VOC) was proposed, which exhibits tunable two-qubit gates mediated by the quadrupole-quadrupole interaction. This system achieves coherence times up to $10 \mu	ext{s}$ at specific 'sweet spots'.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving effective Hamiltonians for the pOv qubit and calculating noise susceptibility using a phenomenological Two-Level Fluctuator (TLF) dipole noise model.

**Model / system.** The system is based on electrons confined in SiGe/Si/SiGe quantum dots, utilizing the coupling between orbital ($	ext{p}_O$) and valley degrees of freedom. The Hamiltonian incorporates Valley-Orbit Coupling (VOC), leading to the pOv qubit subspace.

**Key observables.** Dephasing time ($T_2^*$), two-qubit gate interaction strength (tunable from zero to $\sim 1 	ext{ GHz}$), and noise insensitivity at sweet spots.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Valley splitting energy ($E_v/h \sim 1-10 	ext{ GHz}$); Noise strength ($\delta/E_v \sim 10^{-3}-10^{-4}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats VOC as a perturbation and uses the quadrupole-quadrupole Coulomb interaction for two-qubit gates, neglecting kinetic exchange in some calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SiGe/Si/SiGe heterostructure, how alloy disorder causes valley splittings, and schematic representations of avoided crossings (sweet spots) as a function of dot anisotropy.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the pO qubit to proposing the more complex pOv qubit via VOC. It then analyzes coherence improvement at sweet spots using noise models and finally details the electrically tunable two-qubit gate mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose encoding a qubit in a two-level subspace spanned by the lowest $p$-orbital state in the excited valley of an anisotropic quantum dot and the excited $p$-orbital in the ground valley, which we dub the $pOv$ qubit. There is an avoided crossing between these states due to valley-orbit coupling (VOC) induced by alloy disorder, enabling complete single-qubit control using baseband electrical control of the dot anisotropy. We find that `sweet spots' exist at specific dot orientations where the instantaneous eigenstates are first-order insensitive to charge noise. Using a phenomenological two-level fluctuator (TLF) dipole noise model, we estimate an average dephasing time of $T_2^*\approx 10\,μ\text{s}$ and a quality factor of $Q\sim 10^4$. Alternatively, encoding in the $p$-orbital states in the ground valley near the isotropic dot point, we show that one can induce a similar sweet spot via an out-of-plane magnetic field. Finally, we find that two-qubit gates for the $pOv$ qubit are mediated by the quadrupole-quadrupole Coulomb interaction and can be electrically tuned from zero to $\sim 1~\text{GHz}$ by adjusting the relative orientation of the anisotropic dots, providing a novel pathway towards scalable quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how the dimensionality of CsPbBr3 nanocrystals affects their excitonic structure and light emission. Using a variational effective mass model, the authors find that confinement leads to superradiance, speeding up recombination rates from cubes to rods. The results highlight the crucial role of dielectric confinement and confirm that biexcitons adopt a stable, distorted tetrahedral geometry regardless of crystal shape.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it models complex many-body interactions (electron-hole correlation, polarons) in strongly confined semiconductor nanostructures, providing quantitative predictions for optical response based on geometric control.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically compare the excitonic structure (binding energy and radiative recombination rate) of CsPbBr3 nanocrystals with different dimensionalities: nanorods, nanoplatelets, and nanocubes.

**Main result.** The study shows that superradiance enhances radiative rates from cubes to platelets to rods, and the biexciton geometry is consistently a distorted tetrahedron across all dimensions. Dielectric confinement plays a major role in determining binding energies.

**Method.** A general variational effective mass model incorporating quantum confinement, dielectric confinement, electron-hole correlations, and polaronic effects (Haken model) was employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is CsPbBr3 metal halide perovskite nanocrystals in three dimensions: 1D nanorods, 2D nanoplatelets, and 3D nanocubes. The theory uses a variational effective mass approach to calculate exciton (X) and biexciton (XX) ground state properties.

**Key observables.** Exciton binding energy ($\Delta X$), radiative recombination rate ($k_{rad}^X$), biexciton geometry, electron-hole distances ($r_{eh}$), and carrier-carrier distances ($r_{ee}, r_{hh}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality (1D, 2D, 3D); confinement strength; dielectric mismatch ($\Delta\epsilon$); aspect ratios.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard Bohr radius formulas are insufficient due to complex confinement effects. The analysis for $\Delta XX$ assumes the same dielectric constants for X and XX, potentially underestimating the true value.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare schematics of NCs, NRs, and NPLs; show the effect of anisotropy on electron-hole distance; illustrate the increased e-h distances upon XX formation; and confirm the distorted tetrahedron geometry for XX charge distribution across all shapes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares excitonic properties by first establishing the theoretical framework (variational effective mass model) to calculate key observables like binding energies and radiative rates. It then analyzes how dimensionality dictates these properties, concluding with specific findings on superradiance enhancement and biexciton geometry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study comparing the excitonic ground state properties of CsPbBr$_3$ nanocrystals with different dimensionality: nanorods (1D), nanoplatelets (2D) and nanocubes (3D). All three systems are described on equal footing, by means of a general variational effective mass model, which captures the influence of quantum confinement, dielectric confinement, electron-hole correlations and polaronic effects (within a Haken model). The strongly confined directions squeeze the exciton (X) wavefunction and enhance Coulomb attractions along the weakly confined directions. This stimulates superradiance, thus making radiative recombination rates speed up from cubes to platelets and to rods, in line with recent experiments. The anisotropic local field factor is a secondary, yet non-negligible, mechanism further enhancing radiative rates. X binding energies are also determined primarily by the directions of strong confinenement, which is also consistent with experiments. Weakly confined directions become however influential for small aspect ratios. Dielectric confinement plays a major role in determining the binding energies, and less so in the interparticle-distances. For all dimensionalities, the biexciton (XX) geometry is that of a distorted tetrahedron, rather than squared or linear distributions that would result in Coulomb-governed 2D and 1D structures.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that the transconductance of 2D FETs acts as a spectroscopic tool sensitive to the detailed shape of the hot-carrier energy distribution. By decomposing $g_m$, they isolate an anomalous peak whose existence and parameters allow for all-electrical determination of key hot-carrier properties like energy ($E_0$) and spectral width ($\sigma$). This establishes a powerful, non-optical diagnostic technique for understanding non-equilibrium carrier dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel, purely electrical method for characterizing non-equilibrium carrier energy distributions in nanoscale devices, which is highly relevant for understanding transport limitations in next-generation quantum electronic components.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a spectroscopic probe using transconductance ($g_m$) that measures the *shape* of the carrier energy distribution $f(E)$ in 2D FETs, rather than just its integrated weight (carrier density).

**Main result.** The anomalous peak observed in the shape-driven residual term, $g_m(\alpha)$, confirms that hot carriers introduce spectral features inaccessible to simple density or effective temperature models.

**Method.** The transconductance is decomposed into a conventional density-modulation term and a distribution-shape-driven residual term. This technique is applied using both steady-state DC/lock-in measurements and time-resolved pump-probe spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The study uses two-dimensional (2D) Field-Effect Transistors (FETs), particularly those based on TMDs, where the carrier distribution $f(E)$ is modeled as a combination of thermal and non-equilibrium Gaussian hot-carrier components. The transport physics is analyzed via spectral integrals.

**Key observables.** The anomalous peak in transconductance ($g_m(\alpha)$), drain current ($I_D$), and transient decay times ($	au_E, 	au_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hot-carrier energy ($E_0$), spectral width ($\sigma$), generation threshold ($n_c$), and carrier relaxation time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a gate-independent spectral-kernel approximation, and the hot-carrier distribution is often modeled phenomenologically (e.g., Gaussian bump).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept by contrasting featureless equilibrium responses with anomalous peaks in $g_m(	ext{VG})$ due to hot carriers; time-resolved measurements show characteristic exponential decays allowing separation of energy and density relaxation times.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical decomposition of $g_m$ into shape and density components, demonstrating the anomalous peak signature in steady-state measurements, and finally extending the analysis to time-domain pump-probe experiments to extract dynamic parameters like $	au_E$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The transconductance $g_m = dI_D/dV_G$ of a field-effect transistor (FET) is conventionally read as a proxy for carrier density. We show that it is instead a spectroscopic probe of the carrier distribution: because $g_m$ weights the spectral current $j(E)$ by the gate-voltage derivative $\partial f(E)/\partial V_G$ and integrates over energy, it is sensitive to the \emph{shape} of $f(E)$, not merely its integrated weight $n$. We develop an energy-resolved transport framework for two-dimensional (2D) FETs and, within a gate-independent spectral-kernel approximation, derive the decomposition $g_m = g_m^{(n)} + g_m^{(α)}$ into the conventional density-modulation term $g_m^{(n)}$ and a distribution-shape-driven term $g_m^{(α)}$. The latter, obtained as the residual after subtracting the smooth density-modulation background from the measured $g_m$, exhibits a characteristic anomalous peak at a gate voltage $V_G^{\rm pk}$. This peak has no counterpart in equilibrium transport and \emph{cannot be explained by carrier density modulation alone}. With the spectral kernel calibrated, the peak position and height -- extracted from standard DC/lock-in $g_m$ sweeps -- constrain the hot-carrier energy $E_0$, spectral width $σ$, and generation threshold $n_c$, realizing a steady-state, all-electrical spectroscopy of the carrier distribution. An optional time-resolved extension further recovers the carrier relaxation time $τ$ from the transient response following a pump excitation, establishing the 2D FET as a distribution-function spectrometer that requires no optical readout.
